  \[
    \frac{a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)}{z_m}
    =c_{m\bmod D_N}+O(\theta^m).
  \]
  In particular, either the ratio
  \(a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)/z_m\) decays exponentially fast, or else
  it stays bounded below by a positive constant along an arithmetic
  progression of lengths that contains infinitely many indices with
  \(z_m>0\).
  This theorem is a background conditional-density tool for the stated
  trim strongly connected deterministic setting; the new finite-budget
  use of it is isolated in
  \Cref{cor:relative-finite-budget-obstruction}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  This is the standard conditional-density conclusion for deterministic
  presentations, specialized to our notation; compare
  \cite{HanselPerrin1989,Kozik2005Conditional,Koga2019Density,Montoya2025Relative,BertheGouletPerrin2025}.
  The proof below is included only to identify the constants used later:
  \Cref{lem:substochastic-product-expansion} supplies the product
  expansion, and \Cref{lem:trim-density} supplies the positive
  denominator constants on relevant residue classes.
  Let \(h\in\RR_{>0}^{Q_N}\) be a Perron--Frobenius right eigenvector of
  \(B_N\). By \Cref{lem:substochastic-product-expansion}, there are
  \(D'\ge 1\), non-negative constants \(e_0,\dots,e_{D'-1}\), and
  \(\eta\in(0,1)\) such that
  \[
    a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)
    =\lambda^m\bigl(e_{m\bmod D'}+O(\eta^m)\bigr).
  \]

  Now combine this with \Cref{lem:trim-density}. After replacing \(D'\)
  by a common multiple \(D_N\) of \(D'\) and \(d_N\), and regrouping the
  residue classes, there exist non-negative constants
  \(e_0,\dots,e_{D_N-1}\), \(d_0,\dots,d_{D_N-1}\), and \(\theta\in(0,1)\)
  such that
  \[
    a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)
    =\lambda^m\bigl(e_{m\bmod D_N}+O(\theta^m)\bigr),
    \qquad
    z_m=\lambda^m\bigl(d_{m\bmod D_N}+O(\theta^m)\bigr).
  \]
  If \(z_m>0\) for infinitely many \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_N}\), then
  \Cref{lem:trim-density} gives \(d_r>0\). Along every such residue
  class,
  \[
    \frac{a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)}{z_m}
    =\frac{e_r+O(\theta^m)}{d_r+O(\theta^m)}
    =\frac{e_r}{d_r}+O(\theta^m).
  \]
  Setting \(c_r:=e_r/d_r\) on residue classes with \(d_r>0\) proves the
  stated expansion for all sufficiently large \(m\) with \(z_m>0\).

  Finally, if every relevant \(e_r\) vanishes, then
  \(a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)=O((\lambda\theta)^m)\) on every residue
  class with \(z_m>0\), so the ratio decays exponentially. Otherwise
  some relevant \(e_r\) is positive, and along the corresponding
  arithmetic progression the ratio stays bounded below by a positive
  constant.
\end{proof}

Only the following finite-budget consequence is used in the prime-slice
applications.
It is the exact form of the background relative-density theorem needed
below, after the substochastic product expansion and the positive
periodic denominator constants have already been supplied in
\Cref{lem:substochastic-product-expansion,lem:trim-density}.

\begin{corollary}[Finite-budget lemma for a trim presentation]
  \label{cor:relative-finite-budget-obstruction}
  Let \(\mathcal N\) and \(\mathcal A\) satisfy the hypotheses of
  \Cref{thm:parry-density}. For each \(m\) with \(z_m>0\), let
  \(T_m\subseteq\mathcal N_m\). Suppose that there are constants
  \(\alpha,\beta>0\) such that, for all sufficiently large \(m\) with
  \(z_m>0\),
  \[
    \alpha\,\frac{\lambda^m}{m}
    \le \abs{T_m}
    \le \beta\,\frac{\lambda^m}{m}.
  \]
  Put \(A_m:=L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal N_m\). Then there is a constant
  \(C_{\mathcal A,\mathcal N,T}>0\) such that
  \[
    \abs{A_m\triangle T_m}
    \ge C_{\mathcal A,\mathcal N,T}\,\frac{\lambda^m}{m}
  \]
  for all sufficiently large \(m\) with \(z_m>0\).

  More precisely, after passing to the residue classes supplied by
  \Cref{thm:parry-density}, every relevant residue class has one of the
  following two behaviours. If its limiting relative-density constant
  is zero, then
  \[
    \frac{\abs{A_m\cap T_m}}{\abs{T_m}}
    \le \frac{\abs{A_m}}{\abs{T_m}}
  \]
  decays exponentially along that residue class. If its limiting
  relative-density constant is positive, then along that residue class
  \[
    \abs{A_m\triangle T_m}\gg \lambda^m,
    \qquad
    \frac{\abs{A_m\cap T_m}}{\abs{A_m}}=O(1/m)
  \]
  whenever the second ratio is defined.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Let \(D_N\), \(c_0,\dots,c_{D_N-1}\), and \(\theta\in(0,1)\) be supplied
  by \Cref{thm:parry-density}. Enlarge \(D_N\), if necessary, so that it
  is also a multiple of the period in \Cref{lem:trim-density}. Then for
  every residue class \(r\bmod D_N\) containing infinitely many lengths
  with \(z_m>0\), there is a constant \(d_r>0\) such that
  \[
    z_m=\lambda^m(d_r+O(\theta^m))
    \qquad (m\equiv r\bmod {D_N}),
  \]
  after increasing \(\theta\) within \((0,1)\) if needed. Hence
  \(z_m\asymp\lambda^m\)
  on each relevant residue class.

  Fix such a residue class. If \(c_r=0\), then
  \(\abs{A_m}/z_m=O(\theta^m)\), and therefore
  \[
    \abs{A_m}=O(\lambda^m\theta^m).
  \]
  Choosing \(\eta\) with \(\theta<\eta<1\), the elementary bound
  \(m\theta^m=O(\eta^m)\) gives
  \[
    \frac{\abs{A_m\cap T_m}}{\abs{T_m}}
    \le \frac{\abs{A_m}}{\abs{T_m}}
    =O(m\theta^m)
    =O(\eta^m),
  \]
  so recall against \(T_m\) decays exponentially on this residue class.
  Also, for all sufficiently large \(m\) in this class,
  \[
    \abs{A_m\triangle T_m}
    \ge \abs{T_m}-\abs{A_m}
    \ge \frac{\alpha}{2}\,\frac{\lambda^m}{m}.
  \]

  If \(c_r>0\), then \(\abs{A_m}/z_m\ge c_r/2\) for all sufficiently
  large \(m\equiv r\bmod {D_N}\) with \(z_m>0\). Since
  \(z_m\asymp\lambda^m\) on the residue class, there is a constant
  \(\gamma_r>0\) such that \(\abs{A_m}\ge\gamma_r\lambda^m\). The upper
  bound on \(\abs{T_m}\) then gives, for all sufficiently large \(m\) in
  the class,
  \[
    \abs{A_m\triangle T_m}
    \ge \abs{A_m}-\abs{T_m}
    \ge \frac{\gamma_r}{2}\lambda^m,
  \]
  and, whenever \(\abs{A_m}>0\),
  \[
    \frac{\abs{A_m\cap T_m}}{\abs{A_m}}
    \le \frac{\abs{T_m}}{\abs{A_m}}
    \le \frac{\beta}{\gamma_r}\,\frac{1}{m}.
  \]

  There are only finitely many relevant residue classes. Taking the
  minimum of the positive constants obtained above proves the uniform
  lower bound \(C_{\mathcal A,\mathcal N,T}\lambda^m/m\) for all
  sufficiently large \(m\) with \(z_m>0\), and the two residue-class
  alternatives prove the remaining assertions.
\end{proof}

\noindent
This finite-budget lemma is the only relative-density consequence used
below: the base-\(b\) and Zeckendorf prime slices are exactly the
instances in which \(T_m\) has size comparable to \(\lambda^m/m\) by
\Cref{lem:base-prime-count} and \artref{Lemma~2.5}.

\begin{remark}
  When \(\mathcal N\) is the trim strongly connected presentation of a
  Parry numeration system or of the Zeckendorf language, the same
  presentation-level statement applies with \(\lambda\) equal to the
  dominant root of the corresponding recurrence. Presentations with
  multiple maximal components are covered by
  \Cref{thm:multicomponent-finite-budget}, where polynomial factors are
  retained. Nondeterministic or weighted presentations are not treated
  here; see \cite{CharlierRampersadRigoWaxweiler2010} for examples where
  multicomponent behaviour genuinely occurs.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Relation to conditional-density literature]
  Classical treatments of densities of rational languages already appear
  in Hansel and Perrin~\cite{HanselPerrin1989} and in the conditional
  and relative-density analyses of Kozik~\cite{Kozik2005Conditional},
  Koga \cite{Koga2019Density}, Montoya \cite{Montoya2025Relative}, and
  Berth\'e--Goulet-Ouellet--Perrin \cite{BertheGouletPerrin2025}.
  Thus \Cref{thm:dfa-density-dichotomy,thm:parry-density} are used here
  as standard inputs for the fixed-DFA and strongly connected
  deterministic settings. The multicomponent theorem keeps the
  polynomial factors that those inputs suppress. The contribution is
  the conversion of these residue-class expansions into the
  finite-budget prime obstructions recorded in
  \Cref{thm:multicomponent-finite-budget,cor:relative-finite-budget-obstruction,cor:dfa-prime-symmetric-diff,cor:dfa-prime-recall-precision}.
\end{remark}



[32m succeeded in 321ms:[0m
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Finite-state symbolic models impose rigid constraints on counting
functions. In a deterministic automaton, the number of accepted words
of length \(m\) is governed by powers of a finite matrix. In a sofic
shift, the number of admissible words of a given length is likewise
controlled by a finite-state presentation. These mechanisms force
periodic density laws or exponential-polynomial growth, and that is
often enough to rule out arithmetic languages whose size is governed by
the prime number theorem.

This paper isolates a quantitative finite-budget obstruction. The first
part makes the fixed-base obstruction effective: from the DFA transition
matrix and explicit prime-counting bounds one computes the residue
alternatives, thresholds, and constants in the base-\(b\) prime-slice
comparison. The second part removes the strongly connected restriction
from the relative-density comparison. For an arbitrary trim
deterministic presentation with Perron root \(\lambda>1\), possibly
with several maximal strongly connected components and transient paths
between them, the Perron-scale residue classes have residue-wise
expansions with polynomial factors, while the remaining classes are
sub-Perron. Comparing the active polynomial exponents gives a
finite-budget obstruction for target families of size
\(\asymp\lambda^m/m\) on those active classes. The Zeckendorf
prime-slice results are then the strongly connected specialization of
this mechanism.

The language-theoretic part is independent of the prime number theorem.
After the Zeckendorf context-free argument, it combines the weak pumping
lemma for multiple context-free languages with the eventual affine
recurrence action. Every infinite MCFL then contains one increasing
orbit that returns simultaneously modulo every integer coprime to the
trailing recurrence coefficient. This yields a copy of \(\mathbb Q\) in
the associated deleted-congruence topology, a Cantor closure in the
completion, and an exact Cantor--Bendixson obstruction. Prime and
bounded-\(\Omega\) representation languages are MCF-immune even in the
nonunit case. In a unit recurrence witness, arbitrary-depth quotient
congruences produce an induced countably branching divisibility tree.
The trailing coefficient of a nonminimal witness is not a
numeration-system invariant: \Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation} exhibits
ordinary base \(p\) and an inflated Zeckendorf witness with the same
selected bad-prime set but opposite escape behavior.
Passing instead to the intrinsic minimal tail recurrence yields an exact
inverse statement: bounded prime support away from its constant term
occurs on an infinite MCFL precisely when the canonical language
contains a synchronized ray whose values are an integer geometric
  progression. An explicit linear MCFG generates every such ray. When the
  canonical syntax is supplied by a DFA, positive instances are
  semidecidable. For standard linear Pisot \(U\)-systems the question is
  decidable: a bounded-outside-support MCFL exists exactly when the dominant
  Pisot number is an integer. In particular, for a nonintegral Pisot number
  every infinite MCFL has unbounded support even after the primes dividing
  its norm are deleted. No general decision procedure is asserted beyond
  this linear Pisot class.

For explicit Zeckendorf constants we use the canonical trim strongly
connected presentation, but the abstract finite-budget comparison in
\suppref{Theorem~1.14} applies to arbitrary trim
deterministic presentations. Numeration automata with multiple strongly
connected components do occur
\cite{CharlierRampersadRigoWaxweiler2010}; their polynomial factors are
accounted for in that theorem rather than excluded from the framework.

Throughout, \(f(m)\asymp g(m)\) means that \(c_1g(m)\le f(m)\le c_2g(m)\)
for some positive constants \(c_1,c_2\) and all sufficiently large
\(m\). We use the standard prime-counting convention
\(\pi(x)=\#\{p\le x:p\text{ prime}\}\). Thus an integer half-open
interval \([X,Y)\) contains exactly \(\pi(Y-1)-\pi(X-1)\) primes; in
asymptotic estimates below this may equivalently be written
\(\pi(Y)-\pi(X)+O(1)\). We write \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=1\) for the Fibonacci numbers,
\(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\), and for a Zeckendorf word
\(w=x_1\cdots x_m\) we set
\[
  \val(w):=\sum_{k=1}^m x_kF_{k+1}.
\]
For \(n\ge2\), \(\omega(n)\) denotes the number of distinct rational
prime divisors of \(n\), while \(\Omega(n)\) denotes the total number
of prime factors of \(n\), counted with multiplicity. We put
\(\omega(1)=\Omega(1)=0\), so \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\) for all
\(n\ge1\).

\subsection{Main results}

We separate the classical background inputs from the quantitative
corollaries proved later. The residue-class refinements and their proofs
appear in \suppref{Section~1}; the Zeckendorf results are proved in
\Cref{sec:zeckendorf}.

The logical chain is the following. First,
\suppref{Theorem~1.2} supplies the residue constants \(c_r\)
for a fixed complete DFA, and
\suppref{Theorem~1.14} supplies the multicomponent
finite-budget comparison for general trim deterministic presentations.
Second,
\suppref{Lemma~1.3} and \suppref{Lemma~1.5} turn a published
explicit prime-counting estimate into concrete base-\(b\) slice
constants \(A_b,B_b,M_b\). Third,
\suppref{Theorem~1.7} combines these two
finite inputs and outputs the certified residue alternatives,
thresholds, and symmetric-difference and precision bounds. The later
base-\(b\) and Zeckendorf corollaries are consequences of this
finite-budget conversion.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.18\linewidth}p{0.72\linewidth}}
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning}\\
\(D_A,c_r\) & Period and residue density constants for a complete DFA
\(\mathcal A\) over \(\Sigma_b\).\\
\(\mathcal P_m^{(b)}\) & The base-\(b\) prime slice; we reserve
\(p_m^{(b)}=\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(b)}}\) for its size.\\
\(D_N,d_r,e_r\) & Period and numerator/denominator residue constants
for a trim strongly connected presentation \(\mathcal N\).\\
\(s_r,t_r,S,\lambda\) & Polynomial exponents, maximum exponent, and
Perron root in the multicomponent presentation theorem.\\
\(D_{\mathrm Z},c_r^{(\mathrm Z)}\) & Period and relative-density
constants for the Zeckendorf product presentation.\\
\(\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\), \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) &
The length-\(m\) Zeckendorf prime slice and the full prime Zeckendorf
language.\\
\(A_b,B_b,M_b\), \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z},M_{\mathrm Z}\) &
Explicit prime-slice lower/upper constants and thresholds in the
base-\(b\) and Zeckendorf settings.\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

We begin with the classical density dichotomy for deterministic
automata, recorded in \suppref{Theorem~1.2} because its
constants feed directly into the prime approximation bounds. In the
binary case, if
\[
  a_m(\mathcal A)
  :=\#\{w\in\{0,1\}^m:\mathcal A\text{ accepts }w\},
\]
then
\[
  \frac{a_m(\mathcal A)}{2^m}=c_{m\bmod D_A}+O(\theta^m)
\]
for some automaton period \(D_A\), constants \(c_r\ge0\) indexed by
\(r\bmod D_A\), and \(\theta\in(0,1)\). Thus, on each residue
class, the normalized count either decays exponentially or is eventually
bounded below by a positive constant. In particular, the intermediate scale
\(a_m(\mathcal A)/2^m\asymp m^{-\alpha}\) is excluded.

When this is applied to the binary prime language
\(\mathcal P_m^{(2)}\), whose size is \(\asymp 2^m/m\) by the prime
number theorem \cite{HardyWright2008}, the full base-\(b\) results
\suppref{Corollary~1.10} and \suppref{Corollary~1.12}
give the following binary consequences. For every fixed deterministic
finite automaton there is \(c>0\) such that
\[
  \abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap\{0,1\}^m)\triangle\mathcal P_m^{(2)}}
  \ge c\,\frac{2^m}{m}
\]
for all sufficiently large \(m\), and positive-density languages have
\(\gg 2^m\) symmetric difference along an arithmetic progression. For
the corresponding recall and precision ratios, every fixed DFA has
either exponentially decaying recall or \(O(1/m)\) precision along an
infinite arithmetic progression. These introductory statements are
summaries of the later corollaries, not separate theorem content.

\begin{example}[MSD-first base-\(5\) convention]\label{ex:msd-base}
  With digits read from the most significant position first, the word
  \(w=2031\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}^4\) encodes the integer
  \(2\cdot 5^3+0\cdot 5^2+3\cdot 5+1=266\). The leading digit must be
  non-zero when counting prime slices of fixed length: although the
  word \(0031\) has the same numerical value, it is excluded from
  \(\mathcal P^{(5)}_4\) because it lies outside
  \(\{1,\dots,4\}\Sigma_5^{3}\). This is the convention used throughout
  \suppref{Section~1}.
\end{example}

For the Zeckendorf setting we use the background growth law proved in
the specialized form needed in
\Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}. It applies to regular
sublanguages of
\[
  \mathcal Z
  :=\{x_1\cdots x_m\in\{0,1\}^\ast:
      x_ix_{i+1}\neq 11,\ x_m=1\}.
\]
For such an \(L\), after deleting states that are not reachable from
the initial state or cannot reach an accepting state, let \(\rho\) be
the maximal spectral radius of a strongly connected component in a DFA
recognizing \(L\), and define
\[
  N_L(T):=\#\{w\in L:\val(w)\le T\},
\]
where \(\val(w)\) is the Zeckendorf value of \(w\). The growth law says
\[
  N_L(T)=T^{\alpha+o(1)},
  \qquad
  \alpha=
  \begin{cases}
    0, & \rho\le 1,\\[0.3em]
    \dfrac{\log \rho}{\log \varphi}, & \rho>1.
  \end{cases}
  \qquad (T\to\infty).
\]
\Cref{sec:zeckendorf} combines this background statement with
prime-counting estimates to deduce the quantitative Zeckendorf
slice-obstruction corollaries. Its language-theoretic conclusions are
stronger and do not use the prime number theorem. The regular theorem
\Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping} and its context-free
extension
\Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping} prove, by
block-action pumping modulo \(N_i^2\), that every sufficiently long
word in the respective language class starts an infinite chain
\[
  N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}.
\]
Thus each pumping step may be chosen with quotient coprime to the
previous value, and \(\omega(\val(w))\) is unbounded on every infinite
context-free \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\). The general recurrent-system
results
\Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit,thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint,thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy,thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}
upgrade prime immunity to MCF-immunity and isolate the exact nonunit
escape alternative and its intrinsic geometric-ray inverse. The
  positive semidecidability consequence is
  \Cref{cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable}. In unit systems,
\Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain,thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree}
give arbitrary congruence depth and the induced divisibility tree.
  The specialization and bounded-support classification for standard linear
  Pisot \(U\)-systems are
  \Cref{cor:linear-pisot-prime-cf-immunity,cor:linear-pisot-bounded-support-classification}.
  In the endpoint case \(\rho\le1\), the
displayed formula is used only in the form \(N_L(T)=T^{o(1)}\).

\begin{example}[LSD-first Zeckendorf convention]\label{ex:lsd-zeck}
  Consider the integer \(23=F_8+F_3\). Its Zeckendorf expansion uses
  digits \(x_k\in\{0,1\}\) with \(x_k\) multiplying \(F_{k+1}\) and the
  least significant digit listed first. The admissible word
  \(w=0100001\) has length \(7\), satisfies the no-\(11\) rule, and
  evaluates to
  \(x_1F_2+x_2F_3+\cdots+x_7F_8=0+1\cdot 2+\cdots+1\cdot 21=23\).
  The terminal \(1\) is the most significant digit, consistent with the
  trim deterministic presentation used in \Cref{sec:zeckendorf}.
\end{example}

The final remarks are only contextual. They recall the classical
P\'olya--Carlson--Estermann natural-boundary obstruction for
prime-supported Euler products and the elementary \(2\pi i\)-periodicity
of scalar determinant models after \(z=e^{-s}\); these statements are
not used in the finite-budget proofs.

\subsection{Relation to prior work}\label{subsec:prior-work}

We give a theorem-by-theorem account of what is known, what is a
specialized reproof, and what is new.

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{\suppref{Theorem~1.2} (density dichotomy):
  classical.} The eventual periodicity of normalized word counts for a
  deterministic automaton is a direct Perron--Frobenius consequence and
  is standard in the density literature for regular languages; see
  \cite{Berstel1973,SalomaaSoittola1978,BodirskyGaertnerVonOertzenSchwinghammer2004,Bell2020UpperDensity,Koga2019Density,BerstelReutenauer2011}.
  We record it only in the residue-class form needed for the later
  prime estimates.

  \item \textbf{\suppref{Lemma~1.16} and
  \Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}: classical.} These results are standard residue-class regroupings of
  the periodic Perron--Frobenius theorem for trim strongly connected
  presentations and of the known Zeckendorf growth law; see, e.g.,
  \cite{SalomaaSoittola1978,LecomteRigo2001,CharlierRampersad2011}.

  \item \textbf{Multicomponent theorem.}
  \suppref{Theorem~1.14} keeps the
  residue-wise exponential-polynomial expansion on the Perron-scale
  active classes of arbitrary trim deterministic presentations, absorbs
  lower-growth classes into the sub-Perron term, and compares the
  polynomial exponents in the product automaton. This is the
  presentation-level finite-budget result used to handle maximal
  components connected by transient paths.

  \item \textbf{\suppref{Theorem~1.18} (trim strongly connected
  presentations).} This is the \(s_r=0\) specialization of the
  multicomponent picture and agrees with the conditional-density
  literature
  \cite{HanselPerrin1989,Kozik2005Conditional,Koga2019Density,Montoya2025Relative,BertheGouletPerrin2025}.
  Its role is to isolate the constants used in the Zeckendorf
  application.

  \item \textbf{Effective quantitative prime-slice obstructions.} The
  introductory binary corollaries above summarize the later statements
  \suppref{Proposition~1.9}, \suppref{Corollary~1.10}, and
  \suppref{Corollary~1.12}.
  The effective theorem \suppref{Theorem~1.7}
  makes the fixed-base constants and thresholds computable from a
  compressed Markov-chain certificate for the transition matrix of the
  given DFA. This is an effective packaging of the classical density
  dichotomy with explicit prime-counting input, not a new density
  dichotomy. Together with
  \suppref{Corollary~1.19}, this gives a uniform
  obstruction to approximating the base-\(b\) prime slices by a single
  fixed DFA, both for symmetric difference and for simultaneous
  recall/precision. The Zeckendorf analogues
  \Cref{cor:zeck-symmetric-diff,cor:zeck-recall-precision} follow by the
  same mechanism.

  \item \textbf{Coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping
  (\Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}).} The PNT-free
  unimodular block-action argument is the primary new Zeckendorf
  language theorem in this note. It uses
  \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action,lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence}
  with modulus \(N_i^2\) to build an infinite chain
  \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\) with \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\) inside any
  infinite regular \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\). This implies that new
  rational prime divisors occur along the chain, hence
  \[
    \sup_{w\in L}\omega(\val(w))=\infty.
  \]
  The bounded-distinct-prime and finite-prime-support REG-immunity
  corollaries are the main language-theoretic consequences; bounded
  almost-prime and prime-language REG-immunity are weaker downstream
  cases. The slice-rigidity statement
  \(\Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-slice-rigidity}\) remains a separate
  density-scale obstruction, while the nonregularity corollary
  \(\Cref{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-not-regular}\) follows
  immediately from REG-immunity. The sofic-slice exclusion
  \(\Cref{rem:zeckendorf-primes-not-sofic}\) is then a short consequence
  of regularity of sofic block languages.

  \item \textbf{Context-free, MCFL, and Pisot extensions.}
  Fixed-base prime CF-immunity is classical. Hartmanis and Shank proved
  that no infinite set of primes can be accepted in fixed-base notation
  by a pushdown automaton; Sch\"utzenberger obtained the conclusion
  independently \cite{HartmanisShank1968,Schutzenberger1968}. We do not
  reproduce their proofs as new. Our use of Ogden's lemma
  \cite{Ogden1968} instead couples the two pumped blocks to the
  Zeckendorf unimodular action and yields the stronger coprime-quotient
  chain
  \Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. The
  weak pumping lemma of Seki--Matsumura--Fujii--Kasami
  \cite{SekiEtAl1991} supplies one synchronized \(2k\)-block orbit for
  every infinite \(k\)-MCFL; it is used as an input, not reproved.
  Kanazawa--Kobele--Michaelis--Salvati--Yoshinaka prove that the usual
  strong pumping form fails in general
  \cite{KanazawaKobeleMichaelisSalvatiYoshinaka2014}, and the recent
  substitution lemma of Duncan--Elder--Frenkel--Liu gives a different
  strong interface \cite{DuncanElderFrenkelLiu2026}. Neither result
  concerns recurrence numeration or divisibility. The new deductions
  are the fixed local-congruence orbit
  \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, the topological
  fingerprint \Cref{thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint}, the exact rank
  formula \Cref{prop:local-prime-factor-cb-rank}, and the escape
  dichotomy \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}. The unit-system
  constructions are \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain} and
  \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree}.
  Evertse's theorem on prime ideals in ratios of terms of
  nondegenerate recurrences \cite{Evertse1984} is used as an arithmetic
  input to the geometric-ray characterization
  \Cref{thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}; it is not claimed as
  a new recurrence theorem. The local return congruence further confines the
  geometric ratio to primes dividing the minimal tail constant, and greedy
  length thresholds give the integer/nonintegral Pisot classification in
  \Cref{lem:geometric-ratio-tail-support,cor:linear-pisot-bounded-support-classification}.
  Broughan's \(\mathbb Q\)- and Cantor-space classifications for adic
  integer topologies are cited as classical inputs
  \cite{Broughan2003}. Frougny normalization and the
  Bertrand and substitution literature
  \cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,Fabre1995,FrougnySolomyak1992}
  supply the canonical finite-state numeration interface, not the
  arithmetic pumping proof. The residual nonunit and non-positional
  limits are stated in \Cref{rem:pisot-open-interfaces}. The
  recurrence-witness separation
  \Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation} distinguishes the invariant
  minimal action from an arbitrarily inflated companion presentation.

  \item \textbf{Analytic obstructions: classical background.}
  \suppref{Theorem~A.1} is the usual
  P\'olya--Carlson natural-boundary mechanism for Euler products.
  \suppref{Proposition~A.2} is the corresponding
  periodicity obstruction for determinant models after \(z=e^{-s}\). Both are
  classical
  \cite{Polya1923,Carlson1921,Estermann1928,ArtinMazur1965,BowenLanford1970}.
\end{itemize}
\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Relation to density and approximation literature.} For ordinary
automatic sets, asymptotic density questions go back at least to
Berstel \cite{Berstel1973}; the standard global sources include
B\"uchi's automata-theoretic decidability work \cite{Buchi1960} and
Allouche--Shallit's monograph on automatic sequences
\cite{AlloucheShallit2003}. Matrix and algorithmic treatments appear
in
\cite{BodirskyGaertnerVonOertzenSchwinghammer2004,Bell2020UpperDensity},
and Koga's paper \cite{Koga2019Density} revisits Sin'ya's density-zero
criterion \cite{Sinya2021}. The rational-measure viewpoint of Hansel
and Perrin \cite{HanselPerrin1989} interprets regular languages via
probability measures on shifts of finite type. Kozik's MFCS 2005 paper
\cite{Kozik2005Conditional} formulates conditional densities for
deterministic presentations in the trim strongly connected setting,
Montoya \cite{Montoya2025Relative} recasts these questions in terms of
relative densities, and Berth\'e--Goulet-Ouellet--Perrin
\cite{BertheGouletPerrin2025} establish a broad density theorem under
shift-invariant measures. Accordingly,
\suppref{Theorem~1.2}, \Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}, and
\suppref{Theorem~1.18}
should be read here as background statements in a form suited to the
prime-slice applications, while
\suppref{Theorem~1.14} is the exponent-sensitive
extension needed for general trim deterministic presentations. Cobham's
theorem
\cite{Cobham1969} is the foundational reference for base-dependent
automatic recognizability of sets of integers. Regular approximation of
non-regular languages is a separate line of work; see, for example,
Eisman and Ravikumar \cite{EismanRavikumar2005} and the asymptotic
approximation framework of Sin'ya \cite{Sinya2021}. Polynomial and
sparse growth phenomena for regular languages are part of the classical
transfer-matrix picture \cite{Berstel1973,SalomaaSoittola1978}, and
numeration systems on regular languages are treated in
\cite{LecomteRigo2001,Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,CharlierRampersad2011,Rigo2014Vol1,Rigo2014Vol2}.
The present contribution is narrower: it packages the fixed-DFA
prime-slice obstruction with computable residue-class constants and
explicit thresholds, then applies the same finite-budget argument in the
Zeckendorf setting.


\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Summary.} \suppref{Theorem~1.2 and Lemma~1.16} and
\Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}
record the density statements needed for deterministic presentations.
\suppref{Theorem~1.14} gives the general trim
deterministic finite-budget theorem, including multiple maximal
components, Perron-active residue classes, and their polynomial factors,
and \suppref{Theorem~1.18} records the strongly connected
specialization used for Zeckendorf. The paper's effective contribution
is the finite-budget obstruction mechanism with quantitative fixed-DFA
recall/precision and symmetric-difference instances; see
\suppref{Corollary~1.19}, \suppref{Proposition~1.9},
\suppref{Corollary~1.10}, and \suppref{Corollary~1.12}.
The Zeckendorf analogues are
\Cref{cor:zeck-symmetric-diff,cor:zeck-recall-precision}. The
Zeckendorf prime-language theorem is downstream of the PNT-free
coprime-quotient pumping theorem
\Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. Its principal
consequences are
\Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors,cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
and \Cref{cor:finite-prime-support-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune};
\Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-slice-rigidity} remains a separate
slice-cardinality obstruction, and
\Cref{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-not-regular} is an immediate
consequence of REG-immunity.
Context-free Zeckendorf pumping is
\Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, with the
immunity consequences in
\Cref{cor:zeckendorf-context-free-immunity-package}. The stronger MCFL
package is
\Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit,thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint,prop:local-prime-factor-cb-rank,thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy,thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray,lem:geometric-ratio-tail-support,cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable,thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain,thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree};
its linear Pisot specialization is
\Cref{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity,cor:linear-pisot-prime-cf-immunity,cor:linear-pisot-bounded-support-classification}.
The eventual-sofic exclusion is recorded only as the brief consequence
\Cref{rem:zeckendorf-primes-not-sofic}, while
\suppref{Theorem~A.1} and \suppref{Proposition~A.2}
remain classical background.

\medskip
Finite-automata non-recognizability of the base-\(b\) prime language
goes back to Minsky and Papert \cite{MinskyPapert1966}.
Allen \cite{Allen1968}, Hartmanis--Shank
\cite{HartmanisShank1968}, and Sch\"utzenberger
\cite{Schutzenberger1968} proved stronger related results for broader
language classes and acceptable sets. Shallit's survey and monograph
treat automaticity and number theory from the finite-automata viewpoint
\cite{Shallit1996,AlloucheShallit2003}. Recent work of Yuen and Dubbe
studies regularity and quantitative automaticity around the prime set
\cite{Yuen2022,Dubbe2024}, and Shen proves that prime automata in Yao's
finite-automata primeness model do not exist \cite{Shen2022}. Those
results concern
recognizability or growing-state quantitative automaticity. The present
theorems are different: the automaton is fixed, the comparison is made
on each fixed length slice, and the conclusion is a recall/precision
or symmetric-difference obstruction rather than a state lower bound for
a sequence of automata.

The Zeckendorf theorem in \Cref{sec:zeckendorf} should be compared with,
but not identified with, the classical base-representation results of
Sch\"utzenberger. In a fixed base the digit value update is controlled
by multiplication by the base modulo a prime. For LSD-first Zeckendorf
words the corresponding update is instead the unimodular \(3\times3\)
matrix action on
\((\val(\text{prefix}),F_{r+1},F_{r+2})^t\). This invertibility modulo
the square of the current value is what makes the pumping congruence
work and yields quotients \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\). Thus the quotient
is coprime to the previous value, forcing new rational prime divisors;
the prime-language REG-immunity is the \(K=1\) bounded-distinct-prime
subcase.

The interaction between primes and digital
constraints has a much deeper literature. Mauduit and Rivat
\cite{MauduitRivat2010}, Drmota--Mauduit--Rivat
\cite{DrmotaMauduitRivat2009}, and M\"ullner \cite{Muellner2017} prove
analytic equidistribution results for digit functions and automatic
sequences evaluated at primes. Maynard's theorem on primes with missing
digits \cite{Maynard2019}, Bourgain's work on primes with prescribed
binary digits
\cite{Bourgain2013PrescribedDigits,Bourgain2015PrescribedDigitsII},
Swaenepoel's extension to a positive proportion of preassigned digits
in arbitrary bases \cite{Swaenepoel2020PreassignedDigits}, and
Bhowmik--Suzuki's work on reversed primes in arithmetic progressions
\cite{BhowmikSuzuki2024} address complementary problems about primes
and digital restrictions. These analytic results allow the digit
constraints to vary with the length and prove prime
asymptotics or positive proportions inside those varying families. The
present paper does neither: the automaton is fixed once and for all,
and the obstruction is the elementary mismatch between eventual
periodic finite-state densities and the finite prime budget
\(\asymp\lambda^m/m\). For Zeckendorf and more general Parry numeration
systems, the uniqueness theorem goes back to Lekkerkerker and
Zeckendorf \cite{Lekkerkerker1952,Zeckendorf1972}, and the regularity
of admissible expansions is standard; see
\cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,Rigo2014Vol1,Rigo2014Vol2}. Analytic
work of Drmota--M\"ullner--Spiegelhofer on primes in Fibonacci or
Zeckendorf representations \cite{DrmotaMuellnerSpiegelhofer2021}
studies prime distribution under numeration constraints. Here we use
only the size of the Zeckendorf interval and regular syntax, not an
equidistribution theorem for Zeckendorf digit patterns. The
transfer-matrix viewpoint on word counts is familiar from symbolic
dynamics and analytic combinatorics
\cite{LindMarcus1995,Kitchens1998,FlajoletSedgewick2009}.

Recent Monatshefte work illustrates the surrounding symbolic-dynamical
landscape: Bustos-Gajardo--Kellendonk--Yassawi use finite semigroup
methods for factors of substitution shifts
\cite{BustosKellendonkYassawi2025}, and Charlier--Cisternino study
soficity phenomena in Cantor real-base expansions
\cite{CharlierCisternino2021}. These are complementary to the present
finite-budget obstruction; they do not supply, and are not used as, a
prime-slice density theorem.

For clarity, the comparison with prior work can be summarized as follows.
Regular-language density results of
\cite{HanselPerrin1989,Kozik2005Conditional,Koga2019Density,Montoya2025Relative,BertheGouletPerrin2025}
give the periodic or conditional density statements used here; the new
increment is to feed the resulting residue constants into explicit
prime-slice thresholds for one fixed automaton. Numeration-system work
\cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,CharlierRampersad2011,Rigo2014Vol1,Rigo2014Vol2}
supplies regular admissible languages and growth laws for abstract
numeration, including Zeckendorf-type systems; in the trim strongly
connected cases stated explicitly, the present paper transfers the same
fixed-prime-slice obstruction to Zeckendorf length slices with computable
constants. For primes in Fibonacci or Zeckendorf representations, we use
only the prime number theorem estimate
\(\#\{p\in[F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\}\asymp\varphi^m/m\), and no distribution
theorem for Zeckendorf digit patterns is claimed.

The sofic and orbit-growth papers
\cite{BustosKellendonkYassawi2025,CharlierCisternino2021}, together with
the classical zeta references recalled in \suppref{Appendix~A}, are used
only for contextual comparison: the present argument separates those
phenomena from the fixed prime-slice density obstruction. Similarly,
regular-approximation frameworks for nonregular languages
\cite{EismanRavikumar2005,Sinya2021} position the problem, but the
obstruction proved here is slice-wise and quantitative for one fixed DFA,
not an optimal approximation scheme. The main theorem chain,
\suppref{Theorem~1.7}, \suppref{Theorem~1.14}, and
\suppref{Corollary~1.19},
computes residue alternatives, thresholds, and constants for a fixed
finite automaton, and compares multicomponent polynomial exponents
against a prime budget of order \(\lambda^m/m\).

\subsection{Organisation}

\suppref{Section~1} proves the density dichotomy for deterministic
automata and derives the fixed-base prime corollaries.
\Cref{sec:zeckendorf} records the background growth law for regular
Zeckendorf sublanguages, deduces the corresponding quantitative prime
corollaries, proves the PNT-free coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping
theorem and its unbounded-\(\omega\), bounded-distinct-prime,
finite-prime-support, and prime-language REG-immunity corollaries,
records the older slice-level
rigidity as a separate density obstruction, and then notes the easy
nonregularity and sofic exclusions.
\Cref{sec:context-free-pisot} proves the paired-pump Zeckendorf theorem,
the synchronized MCFL orbit, its local-adic and Cantor--Bendixson
consequences, the nonunit escape dichotomy, the geometric-ray
characterization and its positive semidecidability consequence, and the
unit-system divisibility tree, then specializes the recurrent interface
to linear Pisot \(U\)-systems.
\suppref{Appendix~A} collects final background analytic comparators
concerning Euler products and scalar determinant models. Later sections
cite the statements recorded above directly, so their proofs and
corollaries need not restate the results in full.


[32m succeeded in 311ms:[0m
\section{Context: Zeckendorf Growth}
\label{sec:zeckendorf}

This section specialises the density dichotomy to Zeckendorf words. We
record only the minimal classical facts needed later---in particular the
Zeckendorf growth law---and then combine them with prime-counting
estimates to obtain the quantitative obstructions promised in
\suppref{Section~1}.

Let
\[
  \mathcal Z
  :=\{x_1\cdots x_m\in\{0,1\}^\ast:
      x_ix_{i+1}\neq 11,\ x_m=1\}
\]
be the language of canonical Zeckendorf words. We write these words
least-significant digit first: for \(w=x_1\cdots x_m\in\mathcal Z\),
\[
  \val(w):=\sum_{k=1}^m x_kF_{k+1},
\]
where \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=1\), \(F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n\), and
\(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\). Thus \(x_m=1\) marks the most significant
nonzero digit. The map \(w\mapsto\val(w)\) is a bijection from
\(\mathcal Z\) onto \(\NN_{\ge 1}\) \cite{Zeckendorf1972}.
Consequently \(\mathcal Z\cap\{0,1\}^{\le m}\) parametrises the
interval \([1,F_{m+2})\), so words of length exactly \(m\) parametrise
\([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\). Equivalently,
\(\abs{\mathcal Z_m}=F_m\) for every \(m\ge 1\).
We write
\[
  \mathcal Z_m:=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\abs{w}=m\}
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
  :=\{w\in\mathcal Z_m:\val(w)\text{ is prime}\},
\]
and set
\[
  \mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}
  :=\bigcup_{m\ge 1}\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Length growth for regular Zeckendorf sublanguages]
  \label{lem:zeckendorf-length-growth}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. Choose a DFA recognizing
  \(L\), delete states that are not reachable from the initial state or
  cannot reach an accepting state, and let \(\rho\) be the maximal
  spectral radius of a strongly connected component of the resulting
  automaton. Then \(\rho\le\varphi\). Moreover,
  \[
    \#\{w\in L:\abs w\le m\}=
    \begin{cases}
      \rho^{m+o(m)}, & \rho>1,\\
      \exp(o(m)), & \rho\le1.
    \end{cases}
  \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Since \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), the number of words of length at most
  \(m\) in \(L\) is bounded above by
  \[
    \#(\mathcal Z\cap\{0,1\}^{\le m})=F_{m+2}-1=\varphi^{m+o(m)}.
  \]
  For any trimmed strongly connected component \(C\) of spectral radius
  \(\rho_C\), choose entry and exit paths through the trimmed automaton.
  Periodic Perron--Frobenius theory gives a residue class of lengths on
  which the number of paths that traverse \(C\) is
  \(\rho_C^{n+o(n)}\). These paths yield distinct accepted words after
  adding the fixed entry and exit words. The preceding ambient upper
  bound therefore forces \(\rho_C\le\varphi\) for every component, and
  hence \(\rho\le\varphi\).

  Put the trimmed adjacency matrix in block upper-triangular form by
  strongly connected components. The upper bound
  \(O(m^N\rho^m)\), with \(N\) depending only on the number of blocks,
  follows from the usual path decomposition through this acyclic block
  graph and standard finite-dimensional matrix-power estimates for the
  diagonal blocks. If
  \(\rho>1\), choose a reachable and co-reachable component of spectral
  radius \(\rho\); the same periodic Perron--Frobenius lower bound along
  one admissible residue class gives \(\rho^{m+o(m)}\) words of length at
  most \(m\). If \(\rho\le1\), every non-negative integral irreducible
  recurrent block has spectral radius at most \(1\), hence is either
  nilpotent in the acyclic part or a permutation block on a directed
  cycle. A path can pass through only finitely many such blocks, so the
  total number of accepted words of length at most \(m\) is polynomial in
  \(m\), and therefore \(\exp(o(m))\). This proves the displayed
  estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Growth law for regular Zeckendorf languages]
  \label{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. Choose a DFA recognizing
  \(L\), delete states that are not reachable from the initial state or
  cannot reach an accepting state, and let \(\rho\) be the maximal
  spectral radius of a strongly connected component of the resulting
  automaton. Define
  \[
    N_L(T):=\#\{w\in L:\val(w)\le T\}.
  \]
  If \(L=\varnothing\), then \(N_L(T)=0\) for all \(T\). Otherwise
  \[
    N_L(T)=T^{\alpha+o(1)},
    \qquad
    \alpha=
    \begin{cases}
      0, & \rho\le 1,\\[0.3em]
      \dfrac{\log \rho}{\log \varphi}, & \rho>1.
    \end{cases}
    \qquad (T\to\infty).
  \]
  In the endpoint case \(\rho\le1\) this assertion means only
  \(N_L(T)=T^{o(1)}\); no sharper polynomial asymptotic is asserted.
  This is a transfer-matrix restatement for the LSD-first Zeckendorf
  convention fixed at the start of the section, not a new
  recognizability theorem for abstract numeration systems.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  We use \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-length-growth}. The general
  \(S\)-recognizable-set theorem of
  \cite[Theorem~7]{CharlierRampersad2011} gives the same conclusion. In
  the present system the ambient count satisfies
  \[
    \#(\mathcal Z\cap\{0,1\}^{\le m})
    =F_{m+2}-1
    =\varphi^{m+o(m)},
  \]
  and, conversely, \(F_{m+2}=\varphi^{m+O(1)}\). Hence
  \(\val(w)\le T\) is equivalent, up to an \(O(1)\) error in length, to
  \(\abs w\le (\log T)/(\log\varphi)\).

  By \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-length-growth},
  the number of accepted words of length at most \(m\) is
  \(\rho^{m+o(m)}\) if \(\rho>1\), and \(\exp(o(m))\) if \(\rho\le1\).
  Substituting \(m=(\log T)/(\log\varphi)+O(1)\) gives exactly
  \(N_L(T)=T^{\alpha+o(1)}\), with \(\alpha=0\) for \(\rho\le1\) and
  \(\alpha=\log\rho/\log\varphi\) for \(\rho>1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:zeckendorf-examples}
  The endpoint cases and the borderline case \(\rho=1\) already occur
  for simple regular sublanguages.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
    \item For \(L=\mathcal Z\), one has \(N_L(T)=\lfloor T\rfloor\),
    \(\rho=\varphi\), and \(\alpha=1\).
    \item If \(L\) is finite, then \(N_L(T)\) is eventually constant,
    so \(\alpha=0\).
    \item For \(L=0^\ast1\subseteq\mathcal Z\), one has \(a_m=1\) for
    every \(m\), hence \(\rho=1\) and
    \(N_L(T)\asymp \log T=T^{o(1)}\).
  \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  The same transfer-matrix argument applies more generally to Parry
  numeration systems whose admissible digit language is regular. In that
  setting the regular syntax of admissible expansions replaces
  \(\mathcal Z\), and the dominant exponential growth constant of the
  numeration system replaces \(\varphi\); see
  \cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,LecomteRigo2001,Rigo2014Vol1,Rigo2014Vol2}.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Prime counts in Zeckendorf slices]
  \label{lem:zeck-prime-count}
  Take the explicit prime-counting constants
  \(X_0,C_{\pi,1},C_{\pi,2}\) from \suppref{Lemma~1.3}. There are constants
  \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z}>0\) and an explicit integer \(M_{\mathrm Z}\)
  such that for all \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\),
  \[
    A_{\mathrm Z}\,\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
    \le \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
    \le B_{\mathrm Z}\,\frac{\varphi^m}{m}.
  \]
  Equivalently, for all sufficiently large \(m\), the prime count in
  the exact Zeckendorf slice \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\) is between
  \(c_1F_m/m\) and \(c_2F_m/m\) for suitable constants
  \(c_1,c_2>0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Binet's formula gives
  \[
    F_n=\frac{\varphi^n-(-\varphi)^{-n}}{\sqrt5}.
  \]
  For every \(n\ge2\),
  \[
    \frac{\varphi^n-1}{\sqrt5}\le F_n\le
    \frac{\varphi^n+1}{\sqrt5}\le \varphi^n.
  \]
  Hence, for \(j=1,2\) and \(m\ge1\),
  \[
    c_F\varphi^m\le F_{m+j}\le C_F\varphi^m,\qquad
    c_F:=\frac{\varphi^2-1}{\sqrt5\varphi},\quad
    C_F:=\varphi^2.
  \]
  Define
  \[
    c_{\log}:=\frac12\log\varphi,\qquad C_{\log}:=3\log\varphi,
  \]
  and let \(M_{\log}\) be produced by the following finite calculation.
  Increase \(M\ge2\) until, for all \(m\ge M\) and \(j=1,2\),
  \[
    c_{\log}m\le \log F_{m+j}\le C_{\log}m.
  \]
  This is reproducible from the preceding Binet bounds: the two
  displayed inequalities are guaranteed once the linear inequalities
  \(\log c_F+m\log\varphi\ge c_{\log}m\) and
  \(\log C_F+m\log\varphi\le C_{\log}m\) hold, so it is enough to test
  the resulting explicit lower bounds for \(m\).

  Choose \(M_R\ge M_{\log}\) by the following explicit Binet
  majorization. No monotonicity input is needed for the exact sequence
  \[
    R_m
    :=
    \frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}\,
    \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}.
  \]
  Write
  \[
    F_n=\frac{\varphi^n}{\sqrt5}(1-\varepsilon_n),
    \qquad
    \varepsilon_n:=(-1)^n\varphi^{-2n}.
  \]
  For \(m\ge1\),
  \[
    \frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}
    =
    \varphi^{-1}
    \frac{1-\varepsilon_{m+1}}{1-\varepsilon_{m+2}}
    \le
    \varphi^{-1}
    \frac{1+\varphi^{-2m-2}}{1-\varphi^{-2m-4}},
  \]
  and, once \(\varphi^{-2m-2}\le1/2\),
  \[
    \log F_{m+1}
    \ge
    (m+1)\log\varphi-\tfrac12\log5-2\varphi^{-2m-2}.
  \]
  Also \(F_{m+2}/F_{m+1}\le3\) for \(m\ge1\), so
  \[
    \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}
    =
    1+\frac{\log(F_{m+2}/F_{m+1})}{\log F_{m+1}}
    \le
    1+
    \frac{\log3}
    {(m+1)\log\varphi-\tfrac12\log5-2\varphi^{-2m-2}},
  \]
  whenever the denominator is positive. The two error functions
  \(\varphi^{-2m-2}\) and
  \(((m+1)\log\varphi-\tfrac12\log5-2\varphi^{-2m-2})^{-1}\) are bounded
  above, for all \(m\ge M_{\log}\), by explicit decreasing rational
  majorants obtained from rational lower and upper intervals for
  \(\varphi\) and \(\log\varphi\). Consequently the product of the last
  two displayed upper bounds is itself bounded by a decreasing explicit
  function tending to \(\varphi^{-1}\). A finite rational search on this
  majorant, using rational intervals for \(\varphi,\log\varphi,\log3\),
  produces \(M_R\) such that for every \(m\ge M_R\)
  \[
    \frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}
      \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}
    \le\frac{1+\varphi^{-1}}2 .
  \]
  The certificate therefore verifies a single explicit Binet upper bound
  that is valid for all \(m\ge M_R\), rather than a monotone-tail
  assertion for \(R_m\) itself.

  Choose \(M_0\ge M_R\) by increasing \(M\) until \(F_{m+1}\ge X_0\)
  and
  \[
    \frac12\le
    \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{m\log\varphi},
    \frac{\log F_{m+1}}{m\log\varphi}
    \le2,
  \]
  for every \(m\ge M_0\). The logarithmic ratio conditions are checked
  from the same linear Binet bounds, while the last displayed
  product inequality is already certified by the choice of \(M_R\). Put
  \[
    \Delta_m:=
    1-\frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}
      \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}.
  \]
  Then \(\Delta_m\ge(1-\varphi^{-1})/2\) for \(m\ge M_0\). Enlarge
  \(M_0\) to \(M_{\mathrm Z}\) so that, for all \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\),
  \[
    C_{\pi,1}\left(
      \frac{F_{m+2}}{(\log F_{m+2})^2}
      +\frac{F_{m+1}}{(\log F_{m+1})^2}\right)+2
    \le
    \frac12\,\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}\Delta_m .
  \]
  This last enlargement is also finite and reproducible. Using the
  certified bounds \(c_F\varphi^m\le F_{m+j}\le C_F\varphi^m\) and
  \(c_{\log}m\le\log F_{m+j}\le C_{\log}m\), the left-hand side is
  bounded above by an explicit multiple of \(\varphi^m/m^2\) plus \(2\),
  while the right-hand side is bounded below by an explicit multiple of
  \(\varphi^m/m\). Once that symbolic majorization verifies the tail,
  the finite prefix is checked by exact Fibonacci integers and real
  interval arithmetic for the logarithms. This is the certificate for
  \(M_{\mathrm Z}\).
  Every word in \(\mathcal Z_m\) represents an integer in
  \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\), so
  \[
    \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
    =\pi(F_{m+2}-1)-\pi(F_{m+1}-1)
  \]
  and the interval-count convention following
  \suppref{Lemma~1.3} gives the endpoint correction
  \[
    \left|
      \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}-
      \bigl(\pi(F_{m+2})-\pi(F_{m+1})\bigr)
    \right|\le 2.
  \]
  Here \(\pi(x)=\#\{p\le x:p\text{ prime}\}\). For
  \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\), \suppref{Lemma~1.3} gives
  \[
    \begin{aligned}
    \pi(F_{m+2})-\pi(F_{m+1})
    \ge
    &\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}
      -\frac{F_{m+1}}{\log F_{m+1}}\\
    &{}-C_{\pi,1}\frac{F_{m+2}}{(\log F_{m+2})^2}
      -C_{\pi,1}\frac{F_{m+1}}{(\log F_{m+1})^2}.
    \end{aligned}
  \]
  By the definition of \(M_{\mathrm Z}\), using the displayed endpoint
  inequality,
  \[
    \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
    \ge \frac12\,\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}\Delta_m .
  \]
  The explicit Binet constants above and
  \(\Delta_m\ge(1-\varphi^{-1})/2\) by construction show that one may
  take
  \[
    A_{\mathrm Z}:=\frac{c_F(1-\varphi^{-1})}{8C_{\log}}.
  \]
  For the upper bound,
  \[
    \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}\le \pi(F_{m+2})
      \le C_{\pi,2}\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}
      \le \frac{C_{\pi,2}C_F}{c_{\log}}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}.
  \]
  Thus \(B_{\mathrm Z}:=C_{\pi,2}C_F/c_{\log}\) is admissible for every
  \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\).
\end{proof}

All automata in this section read Zeckendorf words in the displayed
LSD-first order.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical Zeckendorf presentation]
  \label{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation}
  The language \(\mathcal Z\) is accepted by the two-state trim
  strongly connected deterministic presentation \(\mathcal N_{\mathrm Z}\)
  with state set \(\{0,1\}\), initial state \(0\), accepting state
  \(\{1\}\), and transitions
  \[
    0\xrightarrow{0}0,\qquad
    0\xrightarrow{1}1,\qquad
    1\xrightarrow{0}0.
  \]
  Its adjacency matrix is
  \[
    M_{\mathrm Z}=
    \begin{pmatrix}
      1&1\\
      1&0
    \end{pmatrix},
  \]
  its Perron root is \(\varphi\), and its graph period is \(1\). Hence
  \(\mathcal N_{\mathrm Z}\) satisfies the hypotheses of
  \suppref{Theorem~1.18} with \(\lambda=\varphi\), and
  \(z_m=\abs{\mathcal Z_m}=F_m\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  A path in the displayed graph records exactly a binary word with no
  adjacent \(1\)'s: after reading a \(1\) the only available next digit
  is \(0\), while from state \(0\) both digits are available. The
  accepting state is \(1\), so the accepted words are precisely those
  no-\(11\) words whose terminal digit is \(1\), namely
  \(\mathcal Z\). The state \(0\) is initial and lies on the loop
  labelled \(0\), while \(0\xrightarrow{1}1\xrightarrow{0}0\); therefore
  both states are reachable from the initial state and can reach the
  accepting state, and the underlying directed graph is strongly
  connected. The loop at \(0\) gives period \(1\).

  The characteristic polynomial of \(M_{\mathrm Z}\) is
  \(t^2-t-1\). Its spectral radius is the positive root
  \(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2>1\). Finally the accepted length-\(m\) words
  are the canonical Zeckendorf words of length \(m\), whose number is
  \(F_m\) by the standard Fibonacci recurrence with initial values
  \(F_1=F_2=1\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Regular immunity of the Zeckendorf prime language}
\label{subsec:zeckendorf-regular-immunity}

The preceding slice estimates use the prime number theorem. The next
argument is different: it is a PNT-free pumping obstruction in the
spirit of Sch\"utzenberger's finite-state obstruction for base
representations, but adapted to the LSD-first Zeckendorf evaluation.

\begin{lemma}[Unimodular Zeckendorf block action]
  \label{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}
  After reading a prefix of length \(r\) in LSD-first order, put
  \[
    V_r:=
    \begin{pmatrix}
      N_r\\ F_{r+1}\\ F_{r+2}
    \end{pmatrix},
    \qquad
    N_r=\sum_{j=1}^r x_jF_{j+1}.
  \]
  The initial vector is \(V_0=(0,F_1,F_2)^t=(0,1,1)^t\). Reading one
  further digit \(\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}\) sends \(V_r\) to
  \(D_\varepsilon V_r\), where
  \[
    D_\varepsilon:=
    \begin{pmatrix}
      1&0&\varepsilon\\
      0&0&1\\
      0&1&1
    \end{pmatrix},
    \qquad
    \det D_\varepsilon=-1.
  \]
  Hence, for a word \(y=y_1\cdots y_\ell\), the block action
  \[
    M_y:=D_{y_\ell}\cdots D_{y_1}
  \]
  has determinant \((-1)^\ell\). In particular, if \(\ell>0\), then
  the reduction of \(M_y\) belongs to
  \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\) for every \(q\ge2\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  If the next digit is \(\varepsilon\), then it occupies position
  \(r+1\) and contributes \(\varepsilon F_{r+2}\) to the value. Thus
  \[
    N_{r+1}=N_r+\varepsilon F_{r+2},\qquad
    F_{r+2}=F_{r+2},\qquad
    F_{r+3}=F_{r+1}+F_{r+2}.
  \]
  These three identities are exactly
  \(V_{r+1}=D_\varepsilon V_r\). Expanding the determinant along the
  first row gives
  \[
    \det D_\varepsilon
    =\det\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
     +\varepsilon\det\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}
    =-1.
  \]
  Multiplicativity of the determinant gives
  \(\det M_y=(-1)^\ell\). Since \(\pm1\) is a unit modulo every
  \(q\ge2\), the reduction of \(M_y\) is invertible over
  \(\ZZ/q\ZZ\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Zeckendorf pumping congruence]
  \label{lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence}
  Let \(x,y,z\in\{0,1\}^\ast\) with \(\abs y>0\), and put
  \[
    N(k):=\val(xy^kz)
  \]
  whenever the displayed word is read in LSD-first order. If \(q\ge2\)
  and \(h\) is the order of \(M_y\) in
  \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\), then
  \[
    N(k+h)\equiv N(k)\pmod q
  \]
  for every \(k\ge0\). In particular, if \(p=N(k)\) is prime, then
  \(N(k+h)\) is divisible by \(p\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Let \(v_0=(0,1,1)^t\). By
  \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}, concatenation of
  LSD-first blocks gives
  \[
    N(k)=
    e_1^t\,M_zM_y^kM_xv_0,
  \]
  where \(e_1=(1,0,0)^t\). Since \(M_y^h\equiv I\pmod q\), we have
  \(M_y^{k+h}\equiv M_y^k\pmod q\). Multiplying on the left by
  \(M_z\), on the right by \(M_xv_0\), and taking the first coordinate
  gives the congruence for \(N(k+h)\) and \(N(k)\). If \(q=p=N(k)\) is
  prime, this congruence says \(N(k+h)\equiv0\pmod p\).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Prime-factor counts and divisibility antichains]
  \label{def:Omega-total-prime-factor-count}
  For \(n\ge2\), write
  \(n=\prod_p p^{a_p}\). We put
  \[
    \omega(n):=\#\{p:a_p>0\},
    \qquad
    \Omega(n):=\sum_p a_p,
  \]
  where \(\omega(n)\) is the number of distinct rational prime
  divisors of \(n\), and \(\Omega(n)\) is the total number of prime
  factors of \(n\), counted with multiplicity. We also put
  \(\omega(1)=\Omega(1)=0\). Then
  \[
    \omega(n)\le \Omega(n)
  \]
  for every \(n\ge1\). A set
  \(S\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\) is a divisibility antichain if no two
  distinct elements \(a,b\in S\) satisfy \(a\mid b\).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping]
  \label{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be recognized by an \(n\)-state
  deterministic finite automaton. If \(w\in L\), \(\abs w\ge n\), and
  \(\val(w)\ge2\), then there are accepted words
  \[
    w_0=w,\ w_1,\ w_2,\ldots
  \]
  with strictly increasing Zeckendorf lengths and values
  \(N_i:=\val(w_i)\) such that, for every \(i\ge0\),
  \[
    N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}.
  \]
  In particular \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\) for every \(i\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Let \(\mathcal A\) be an \(n\)-state DFA recognizing \(L\). Since
  \(\mathcal A\) accepts \(w\) and \(\abs w\ge n\), the usual pumping
  argument gives a factorization \(w=xyz\) with \(\abs y>0\) and
  \(xy^kz\in L\) for every \(k\ge0\). Put
  \[
    k_0=1,\qquad w_i=xy^{k_i}z,\qquad
    N_i=\val(w_i).
  \]
  Thus \(w_0=w\) and \(N_0=\val(w)\ge2\). Suppose \(k_i\) has been
  defined and \(N_i\ge2\). Let
  \[
    h_i=
    \operatorname{ord}_{\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/N_i^2\ZZ)}(M_y)
  \]
  be the order of the nonempty middle-block matrix modulo \(N_i^2\),
  which is defined by
  \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}. Set
  \[
    k_{i+1}=k_i+h_i .
  \]
  Applying \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence} with
  \(q=N_i^2\) gives
  \[
    N_{i+1}
      =\val(xy^{k_{i+1}}z)
      \equiv \val(xy^{k_i}z)
      =N_i
      \pmod {N_i^2}.
  \]
  Hence there is an integer \(t_i\) with
  \[
    N_{i+1}=N_i+N_i^2t_i=N_i(1+N_it_i).
  \]
  Since \(h_i\ge1\) and \(\abs y>0\), we have \(k_{i+1}>k_i\), and
  the length of \(xy^{k_{i+1}}z\) is strictly larger than the length
  of \(xy^{k_i}z\). Both words lie in \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), hence
  are canonical Zeckendorf words. The canonical words of length \(m\)
  represent exactly the interval \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\), and these
  intervals are strictly ordered by length. Therefore
  \(N_{i+1}>N_i\). It follows from the displayed formula that
  \(t_i\ge1\).

  Put \(Q_i=1+N_it_i\). Then \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\),
  \(Q_i>1\), and \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\). The last congruence gives
  \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\). The strict growth also gives
  \(N_{i+1}\ge N_i\ge2\), so the recursive construction continues
  indefinitely. This proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Zeckendorf divisibility-chain pumping]
  \label{thm:zeckendorf-divisibility-chain-pumping}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be recognized by an \(n\)-state
  deterministic finite automaton. If \(w\in L\), \(\abs w\ge n\), and
  \(\val(w)\ge2\), then there are words
  \[
    w_0=w,\ w_1,\ w_2,\ldots
  \]
  in \(L\) such that the Zeckendorf lengths \(\abs{w_i}\) are strictly
  increasing and
  \[
    \val(w_i)\ \text{strictly divides}\ \val(w_{i+1})
  \]
  for every \(i\ge0\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  This is the immediate divisibility part of
  \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}: there
  \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\) with \(Q_i>1\), so \(N_i\) strictly divides
  \(N_{i+1}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[New rational primes in every infinite regular Zeckendorf language]
  \label{cor:new-rational-primes-in-every-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-language}
  If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then \(L\)
  contains accepted words \(w_0,w_1,\ldots\) such that, with
  \(N_i=\val(w_i)\), the set of rational prime divisors of \(N_i\)
  strictly increases at every step. More precisely, in the chain of
  \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, every quotient
  \(Q_i=N_{i+1}/N_i\) has a prime divisor that divides no earlier
  \(N_j\), \(0\le j\le i\). Hence infinitely many distinct rational
  primes divide values occurring in \(L\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Let an \(n\)-state DFA recognize \(L\). Since \(L\) is infinite, it
  contains words of arbitrarily large length; all but at most one have
  value at least \(2\). Choose \(w\in L\) with \(\abs w\ge n\) and
  \(\val(w)\ge2\), and apply
  \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. For each \(i\),
  \(Q_i>1\), so choose a prime \(p_i\mid Q_i\). Since
  \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\), we have \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\). Moreover
  \(N_j\mid N_i\) for every \(j\le i\), because the values form a
  divisibility chain. Thus \(p_i\) divides none of
  \(N_0,\ldots,N_i\). But \(p_i\mid N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\), so the prime
  divisor set strictly increases from \(N_i\) to \(N_{i+1}\). Iterating
  gives infinitely many distinct rational prime divisors among the
  accepted values.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Unbounded distinct prime divisors]
  \label{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}
  If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then
  \[
    \sup_{w\in L}\omega(\val(w))=\infty .
  \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  In the sequence supplied by
  \Cref{cor:new-rational-primes-in-every-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-language},
  each step introduces at least one rational prime divisor not present
  earlier. Hence
  \[
    \omega(\val(w_i))\ge \omega(\val(w_0))+i
  \]
  for every \(i\), and the displayed supremum is infinite.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Unbounded prime-factor count in infinite regular Zeckendorf languages]
  \label{cor:unbounded-prime-factor-count-in-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-languages}
  If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then
  \[
    \sup_{w\in L}\Omega(\val(w))=\infty .
  \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  This follows from
  \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors} and the inequality
  \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Divisibility-antichain obstruction]
  \label{cor:divisibility-antichain-obstruction}
  If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is regular and \(\val(L)\) is a
  divisibility antichain, then \(L\) is finite.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  If \(L\) were infinite, then it would contain an accepted word
  \(w\) of length at least the number of states in a DFA for \(L\) and
  with \(\val(w)\ge2\). The
  divisibility-chain theorem would produce \(w_0,w_1\in L\) with
  \(\val(w_0)\) strictly dividing \(\val(w_1)\), contradicting the
  antichain hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Bounded-distinct-prime Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
  \label{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
  For every fixed \(K\ge1\), the language
  \[
    \mathcal B_K
      :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\omega(\val(w))\le K\}
  \]
  is infinite and contains no infinite regular sublanguage.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  The language \(\mathcal B_K\) is infinite because it contains the
  Zeckendorf representations of all primes. If \(L\) were an infinite
  regular sublanguage of \(\mathcal B_K\), then
  \(\omega(\val(w))\le K\) for every \(w\in L\), contradicting
  \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite-prime-support Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
  \label{cor:finite-prime-support-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
  Let \(S\) be a finite set of rational primes and put
  \[
    \mathcal Z_S
      :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:
          \text{every prime divisor of }\val(w)\text{ lies in }S\}.
  \]
  Then \(\mathcal Z_S\) contains no infinite regular sublanguage. If
  \(S\neq\emptyset\), then \(\mathcal Z_S\) is infinite, and therefore
  it is REG-immune.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Every value represented in \(\mathcal Z_S\) has at most \(\abs S\)
  distinct prime divisors. Thus any infinite regular sublanguage of
  \(\mathcal Z_S\) would contradict
  \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}. If \(S\neq\emptyset\),
  choose \(p\in S\). For every \(a\ge0\), the integer \(p^a\) has all
  its prime divisors in \(S\), and its canonical Zeckendorf
  representation belongs to \(\mathcal Z_S\). These values are
  distinct, so \(\mathcal Z_S\) is infinite.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Bounded almost-prime Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
  \label{cor:bounded-almost-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
  For every fixed \(K\ge1\), the language
  \[
    \mathcal A_K
      :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\Omega(\val(w))\le K\}
  \]
  is infinite and contains no infinite regular sublanguage.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  The language \(\mathcal A_K\) is infinite because it contains the
  Zeckendorf representations of all primes. Since
  \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\), we have
  \(\mathcal A_K\subseteq\mathcal B_K\). Hence an infinite regular
  sublanguage of \(\mathcal A_K\) would be an infinite regular
  sublanguage of \(\mathcal B_K\), contradicting
  \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf-Sch\"utzenberger regular immunity]
  \label{thm:zeckendorf-schutzenberger-regular-immunity}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. If
  \[
    \val(L):=\{\val(w):w\in L\}
  \]
  is contained in the set of prime numbers, then \(L\) is finite. More
  quantitatively, if an \(n\)-state deterministic finite automaton
  recognizes such an \(L\), then no word of length at least \(n\) is
  accepted.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Since prime values have exactly one distinct prime divisor, the
  finiteness assertion follows from
  \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
  with \(K=1\). For the quantitative statement, suppose an \(n\)-state
  DFA accepting such an \(L\) accepted a word \(w\) with \(\abs w\ge n\).
  Then \(\val(w)\) is prime, hence at least \(2\). By
  \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, \(L\) would contain
  accepted values \(N_0\) and \(N_1=N_0Q_0\) with \(Q_0>1\), so
  \(N_1\) is composite. This contradicts the assumption that
  \(\val(L)\) is contained in the primes.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Infinite regular Zeckendorf languages contain composites]
  \label{cor:regular-zeckendorf-languages-contain-composites}
  Every infinite regular language \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) contains
  infinitely many words whose Zeckendorf values are composite.
  Equivalently, no fixed DFA can generate infinitely many canonical
  Zeckendorf primes without also generating infinitely many composite
  values.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  By
  \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors},
  the set of values \(\omega(\val(w))\) is unbounded on \(L\). In
  particular, infinitely many accepted words have
  \(\omega(\val(w))\ge2\), and their values are composite.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The Zeckendorf prime language is REG-immune]
  \label{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-reg-immune}
  The language
  \[
    \mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}
    =\{w\in\mathcal Z:\val(w)\text{ is prime}\}
  \]
  is REG-immune: it is infinite and contains no infinite regular
  sublanguage.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Euclid's theorem and the bijection \(w\mapsto\val(w)\) from
  \(\mathcal Z\) to \(\NN_{\ge1}\) show that
  \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is infinite. If it contained an infinite
  regular sublanguage \(L\), then every value in \(L\) would have
  exactly one distinct prime divisor, contradicting
  \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
  with \(K=1\).
\end{proof}

For a deterministic automaton \(\mathcal A\) over the Zeckendorf digit
alphabet, write
\[
  a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)
  :=\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m}.
\]

\begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf symmetric-difference obstruction]
  \label{cor:zeck-symmetric-diff}
  All automata in this statement read the Zeckendorf words LSD-first, as
  in the definition of \(\mathcal Z\) above.
  Let \(\mathcal A\) be a deterministic automaton over the Zeckendorf
  alphabet. Then there exists \(c_{\mathcal A,\varphi}>0\) such that
  \[
    \abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m)
         \triangle\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
    \ge c_{\mathcal A,\varphi}\,\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
  \]
  for all sufficiently large \(m\). If
  \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)\) lies in the positive-density
  alternative of \suppref{Theorem~1.18}, then along the corresponding
  arithmetic progression of lengths one has the stronger bound
  \(\abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m)\triangle\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
  \gg \varphi^m\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Apply \suppref{Corollary~1.19} with
  \[
    \mathcal N=\mathcal N_{\mathrm Z},\qquad
    \lambda=\varphi,\qquad
    z_m=F_m,\qquad
    T_m=\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}.
  \]
  The presentation hypotheses and \(z_m=F_m\) are supplied by
  \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation}; the finite-budget
  hypothesis
  \[
    A_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
    \le \abs{T_m}
    \le B_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
  \]
  is \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}. The corollary gives the all-large-\(m\)
  symmetric-difference bound. Its positive-density residue-class clause
  gives the stronger \(\Omega(\varphi^m)\) bound on every residue class
  where the limiting relative density of
  \(L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m\) inside \(\mathcal Z_m\) is positive.
\end{proof}

Define recall and precision for Zeckendorf slices by
\[
  \mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
  :=\frac{\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
          {\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}},
  \qquad
  \mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
  :=\frac{\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
          {a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}
\]
whenever both \(\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}>0\) and
\(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)>0\), i.e. on lengths where the
denominator is nonzero.

\begin{corollary}[No simultaneous recall and precision in Zeckendorf]
  \label{cor:zeck-recall-precision}
  Let \(\mathcal A\) be a deterministic automaton over the Zeckendorf
  alphabet. Let \(D_Z\), the constants \(c_0,\dots,c_{D_Z-1}\), and
  \(\theta\) be supplied by
  \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation} and \suppref{Theorem~1.18}, so that
  \[
    \frac{a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}{F_m}
    =c_{m\bmod D_Z}+O(\theta^m)
  \]
  for all sufficiently large \(m\). Let \(B_{\mathrm Z}\) be the upper
  estimate constant from \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}. For each residue
  class \(r\bmod D_Z\), exactly one of the following alternatives holds:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
    \item If \(c_r=0\), then
    \(\mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\) decays exponentially along
    \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\).
    \item If \(c_r>0\), then there is a constant
    \(C_{r,\varphi}>0\), for instance
    \(C_{r,\varphi}=2C_\varphi B_{\mathrm Z}/c_r\) with
    \(F_m\ge C_\varphi^{-1}\varphi^m\) for all large \(m\), such that
    \[
      \mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\le \frac{C_{r,\varphi}}{m}
    \]
    for all sufficiently large \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\).
  \end{enumerate}
  Consequently every deterministic automaton satisfies one of the
  following statements:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
    \item \(\mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\) decays exponentially along
    every residue class of lengths.
    \item There exists at least one residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\) with
    \(c_r>0\) such that
    \(\mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\le C_{\mathcal A,\varphi}/m\) for
    all sufficiently large \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\) for which precision is
    defined.
  \end{enumerate}
  Precision is left undefined on lengths with
  \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)=0\); the statement makes no uniform
  precision assertion on residue classes other than those covered by
  the per-residue alternative.
  Consequently no fixed DFA has both recall and precision bounded below
  on infinitely many Zeckendorf lengths.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Fix \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z}>0\) as in
  \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}; thus
  \[
    A_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
    \le \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
    \le B_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
  \]
  for all sufficiently large \(m\). Also fix constants
  \(c_F',C_F'>0\) such that
  \(c_F'\varphi^m\le F_m\le C_F'\varphi^m\) for all large \(m\).

  Treat each residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\) separately. If \(c_r=0\), then
  along \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\),
  \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)=O(\theta^mF_m)\). Hence
  \[
    \mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
    \le
    \frac{a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}
         {\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
    \ll m\theta^m.
  \]
  Since \(m\theta^m=O(\theta_r^m)\) for some \(\theta_r\in(0,1)\),
  recall decays exponentially on this residue class.

  If \(c_r>0\), then for all sufficiently large \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\),
  \[
    a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)\ge \frac{c_r}{2}F_m
    \ge \frac{c_rc_F'}{2}\varphi^m.
  \]
  Therefore
  \[
    \mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
    \le
    \frac{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
         {a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}
    \le
    \frac{2B_{\mathrm Z}}{c_rc_F'}\cdot\frac{1}{m}.
  \]
  This proves the stated per-residue alternative, with
  \(C_{r,\varphi}=2B_{\mathrm Z}/(c_rc_F')\).

  If all \(c_r\) vanish, the first alternative holds on every residue
  class, hence globally. Otherwise choose a residue class with
  \(c_r>0\); the second alternative holds on defined lengths in that
  arithmetic progression, and one may take
  \(C_{\mathcal A,\varphi}=C_{r,\varphi}\).

  Finally suppose that both recall and precision were bounded below by
  some \(\delta>0\) on infinitely many Zeckendorf lengths. By the
  pigeonhole principle, infinitely many of those lengths lie in one
  residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\). The case \(c_r=0\) contradicts the
  exponential recall decay above. The case \(c_r>0\) contradicts the
  bound \(\mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\le C_{r,\varphi}/m\) along that
  same residue class.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Regular Zeckendorf slices exclude the prime scale]
  \label{prop:zeckendorf-regular-slice-rigidity}
  Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be a regular language, and put
  \[
    \ell_m:=\abs{L\cap\mathcal Z_m}.
  \]
  Then there are an integer \(D_Z\ge1\), non-negative constants
  \(c_0,\dots,c_{D_Z-1}\), and \(\theta\in(0,1)\) such that
  \[
    \frac{\ell_m}{F_m}=c_{m\bmod D_Z}+O(\theta^m).
  \]
  Consequently, on each residue class modulo \(D_Z\), the ratio
  \[
    \frac{\ell_m}{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
  \]
  either tends to \(0\) exponentially up to the harmless polynomial
  factor \(m\), or tends to \(+\infty\) at least linearly in \(m\).
  In particular no regular sublanguage of \(\mathcal Z\) has
  \(\ell_m=\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}\) for all sufficiently
  large \(m\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Let \(\mathcal A\) be a deterministic automaton recognizing \(L\).
  Since \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), one has
  \[
    \ell_m=\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m}
          =a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A).
  \]
  By \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation}, the canonical
  Zeckendorf presentation is trim, deterministic, strongly connected,
  has Perron root \(\varphi\), and satisfies
  \(z_m=\abs{\mathcal Z_m}=F_m\). Applying
  the background relative-density theorem
  \suppref{Theorem~1.18} to this presentation and to
  \(\mathcal A\) gives the displayed expansion; its proof is the
  substochastic product expansion of
  \suppref{Lemma~1.17} combined with the positive
  periodic denominator constants from \suppref{Lemma~1.16}.

  Fix a residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\). By \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}
  there are constants \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z}>0\) such that
  \[
    A_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
    \le\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
    \le B_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
  \]
  for all sufficiently large \(m\). Also fix \(c_F'',C_F''>0\) such that
  \(c_F''\varphi^m\le F_m\le C_F''\varphi^m\) for all large \(m\).

  If \(c_r=0\), then along \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\) we have
  \[
    \ell_m=O(\theta^mF_m)=O(\theta^m\varphi^m).
  \]
  Hence
  \[
    \frac{\ell_m}{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
    =O(m\theta^m),
  \]
  which tends to \(0\) exponentially after increasing \(\theta\) within
  \((0,1)\).

  If \(c_r>0\), then for all sufficiently large
  \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\),
  \[
    \ell_m\ge\frac{c_r}{2}F_m
        \ge\frac{c_rc_F''}{2}\varphi^m.
  \]
  Therefore
  \[
    \frac{\ell_m}{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
    \ge
    \frac{c_rc_F''}{2B_{\mathrm Z}}\,m,
  \]
  and the ratio tends to \(+\infty\) at least linearly on this residue
  class.

  Every residue class falls into exactly one of these two cases. Thus
  equality
  \(\ell_m=\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}\) cannot hold for all
  sufficiently large \(m\), since any fixed residue class contains
  arbitrarily large lengths and on that class the ratio cannot remain
  equal to \(1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Prime Zeckendorf language is not regular]
  \label{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-not-regular}
  The prime Zeckendorf language \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is not
  regular.
\end{corollary}

This corollary is now an immediate consequence of the stronger
PNT-free regular-immunity theorem. The preceding
\Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-slice-rigidity} remains useful as a
separate slice-scale obstruction: it explains why the exact
prime-slice cardinalities cannot be reproduced by a regular
sublanguage on all large lengths. General results relating
\(S\)-recognizability, \(S\)-automaticity, and morphic predicates in
abstract numeration systems include Maes--Rigo \cite{MaesRigo2002} and
the standard references cited in \Cref{subsec:prior-work}.

\begin{proof}
  This follows at once from
  \Cref{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-reg-immune}, since an infinite
  regular language is an infinite regular sublanguage of itself.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Sofic corollary]
  \label{rem:zeckendorf-primes-not-sofic}
  The preceding non-regularity statement excludes eventual sofic
  realization of the prime Zeckendorf length slices by the following
  short argument.
  Let \(\mathcal B_m(X)\) denote the admissible words of length
  \(m\) in a sofic shift \(X\). If
  \(\mathcal B_m(X)=\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\) for all \(m\ge N\),
  then
  \[
    \mathcal B(X)\triangle\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\subseteq \{0,1\}^{<N},
  \]
  a finite set. Since \(\mathcal B(X)\) is regular
  (\cite[Theorem~3.2.1]{LindMarcus1995}; see also
  \cite[Chapter~4]{PerrinPin2004}), this would make
  \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) a finite symmetric difference away from a
  regular language, hence regular itself, contradicting
