\section{Further remarks}\label{sec:conclusion}

This paper proves a finite-budget obstruction for fixed finite-state
models facing prime-scale slices. The effective fixed-DFA theorem makes
the base-\(b\) obstruction computable from the transition matrix: the
residue alternatives, thresholds, and constants come from finite
Markov-chain linear algebra plus explicit prime-counting bounds. The
multicomponent presentation theorem keeps the residue-wise polynomial
factors on \(\lambda\)-active classes of arbitrary trim deterministic
presentations and compares those exponents against targets of size
\(\asymp\lambda^m/m\).

Together these results show that fixed-DFA approximation of prime
slices fails quantitatively: recall and precision cannot stay
simultaneously bounded below, and the symmetric difference from the
prime slice is always at least \(c_{\mathcal A,b}b^m/m\) in the ordinary
base-\(b\) setting and at least
\(c_{\mathcal A,\varphi}\varphi^m/m\) in the Zeckendorf setting. The
  Zeckendorf section also proves a sharper PNT-free language-theoretic
  obstruction. The unimodular action modulo \(N_i^2\) works for the two
  simultaneous pumps supplied by Ogden's lemma and gives the stated
  context-free coprime-quotient chain.

The same calculation depends only on a stationary affine companion
action. Under unique canonical representation, strict length ordering,
and an eventual integral recurrence with nonzero trailing coefficient,
the weak MCFL pumping lemma supplies one increasing orbit that is
periodic modulo every integer coprime to the trailing coefficient. Its
deleted-congruence subspace is a copy of \(\mathbb Q\), and its closure
in the corresponding profinite completion is a Cantor set. The exact
Cantor--Bendixson rank of bounded local prime-factor layers gives the
recurrence-witness-relative nonunit dichotomy: every infinite MCFL has
unbounded prime support away from the trailing coefficient or unbounded
valuation at one of its prime divisors. Thus prime and bounded-\(\Omega\) languages are
MCF-immune for all systems in the recurrent interface.

The selected trailing coefficient need not be intrinsic. In particular,
inflating the Fibonacci recurrence by \(X-p\) produces the
same selected bad-prime set as base \(p\) without producing its escape
language.

For the intrinsic minimal tail recurrence, the residual escape question
has an exact structural answer. An infinite MCFL with bounded prime
support away from the minimal constant term exists if and only if the
canonical language contains a synchronized pumping ray whose numerical
values are \(cb^t\) with integers \(c\ge1\) and \(b\ge2\). Such a ray has
an explicit finite-fan-out linear MCFG. With a DFA for the canonical
syntax, candidate rays can be enumerated and checked by finite-monoid
periodicity and exact recurrence identities, giving positive
semidecidability. The ratio of every certified geometric ray is supported on
the minimal tail constant. For standard linear Pisot \(U\)-systems, greedy
length thresholds complete the classification: such a ray, and hence such an
MCFL, exists exactly when the dominant Pisot number is an integer. Thus this
subclass is decidable by inspecting the degree of the minimal polynomial.
Termination on negative instances for general regular recurrent
presentations remains open.

In a unit recurrence, the same fixed orbit realizes quotient
congruences of arbitrary prescribed depth. A recursive use of these
congruences embeds the full countably branching tree as an induced
divisibility suborder, with pairwise coprime edge quotients whose least
prime factors exceed arbitrary edge-wise thresholds. Standard linear
Pisot \(U\)-systems satisfy the recurrent interface; Frougny
normalization supplies their finite-state canonical syntax. In the
nonunit case, ordinary base-\(b\) powers show that the valuation escape
alternative is necessary, whereas every nonintegral Pisot system forces
unbounded support away from the norm primes. The final analytic appendix is only
contextual comparison.

Several natural questions remain outside the scope of this paper. The
density and finite-budget results use determinism essentially;
nondeterministic, weighted, or
bounded-ambiguity variants would require new hypotheses because
cancellation or ambiguity can change the counting problem. Broader
arithmetic targets other than primes are likewise future-work
directions rather than theorem claims here. Arbitrary real-base
\(\beta\)-shifts, redundant representations, and non-positional
abstract numeration systems also remain outside the theorem unless they
can be equipped with the stationary invertible block action isolated in
\Cref{rem:pisot-open-interfaces}.


[32m succeeded in 203ms:[0m
 401: \subsection{Regular immunity of the Zeckendorf prime language}
 402: \label{subsec:zeckendorf-regular-immunity}
 403: 
 404: The preceding slice estimates use the prime number theorem. The next
 405: argument is different: it is a PNT-free pumping obstruction in the
 406: spirit of Sch\"utzenberger's finite-state obstruction for base
 407: representations, but adapted to the LSD-first Zeckendorf evaluation.
 408: 
 409: \begin{lemma}[Unimodular Zeckendorf block action]
 410:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}
 411:   After reading a prefix of length \(r\) in LSD-first order, put
 412:   \[
 413:     V_r:=
 414:     \begin{pmatrix}
 415:       N_r\\ F_{r+1}\\ F_{r+2}
 416:     \end{pmatrix},
 417:     \qquad
 418:     N_r=\sum_{j=1}^r x_jF_{j+1}.
 419:   \]
 420:   The initial vector is \(V_0=(0,F_1,F_2)^t=(0,1,1)^t\). Reading one
 421:   further digit \(\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}\) sends \(V_r\) to
 422:   \(D_\varepsilon V_r\), where
 423:   \[
 424:     D_\varepsilon:=
 425:     \begin{pmatrix}
 426:       1&0&\varepsilon\\
 427:       0&0&1\\
 428:       0&1&1
 429:     \end{pmatrix},
 430:     \qquad
 431:     \det D_\varepsilon=-1.
 432:   \]
 433:   Hence, for a word \(y=y_1\cdots y_\ell\), the block action
 434:   \[
 435:     M_y:=D_{y_\ell}\cdots D_{y_1}
 436:   \]
 437:   has determinant \((-1)^\ell\). In particular, if \(\ell>0\), then
 438:   the reduction of \(M_y\) belongs to
 439:   \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\) for every \(q\ge2\).
 440: \end{lemma}
 441: 
 442: \begin{proof}
 443:   If the next digit is \(\varepsilon\), then it occupies position
 444:   \(r+1\) and contributes \(\varepsilon F_{r+2}\) to the value. Thus
 445:   \[
 446:     N_{r+1}=N_r+\varepsilon F_{r+2},\qquad
 447:     F_{r+2}=F_{r+2},\qquad
 448:     F_{r+3}=F_{r+1}+F_{r+2}.
 449:   \]
 450:   These three identities are exactly
 451:   \(V_{r+1}=D_\varepsilon V_r\). Expanding the determinant along the
 452:   first row gives
 453:   \[
 454:     \det D_\varepsilon
 455:     =\det\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
 456:      +\varepsilon\det\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}
 457:     =-1.
 458:   \]
 459:   Multiplicativity of the determinant gives
 460:   \(\det M_y=(-1)^\ell\). Since \(\pm1\) is a unit modulo every
 461:   \(q\ge2\), the reduction of \(M_y\) is invertible over
 462:   \(\ZZ/q\ZZ\).
 463: \end{proof}
 464: 
 465: \begin{lemma}[Zeckendorf pumping congruence]
 466:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence}
 467:   Let \(x,y,z\in\{0,1\}^\ast\) with \(\abs y>0\), and put
 468:   \[
 469:     N(k):=\val(xy^kz)
 470:   \]
 471:   whenever the displayed word is read in LSD-first order. If \(q\ge2\)
 472:   and \(h\) is the order of \(M_y\) in
 473:   \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\), then
 474:   \[
 475:     N(k+h)\equiv N(k)\pmod q
 476:   \]
 477:   for every \(k\ge0\). In particular, if \(p=N(k)\) is prime, then
 478:   \(N(k+h)\) is divisible by \(p\).
 479: \end{lemma}
 480: 
 481: \begin{proof}
 482:   Let \(v_0=(0,1,1)^t\). By
 483:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}, concatenation of
 484:   LSD-first blocks gives
 485:   \[
 486:     N(k)=
 487:     e_1^t\,M_zM_y^kM_xv_0,
 488:   \]
 489:   where \(e_1=(1,0,0)^t\). Since \(M_y^h\equiv I\pmod q\), we have
 490:   \(M_y^{k+h}\equiv M_y^k\pmod q\). Multiplying on the left by
 491:   \(M_z\), on the right by \(M_xv_0\), and taking the first coordinate
 492:   gives the congruence for \(N(k+h)\) and \(N(k)\). If \(q=p=N(k)\) is
 493:   prime, this congruence says \(N(k+h)\equiv0\pmod p\).
 494: \end{proof}
 495: 
 496: \begin{definition}[Prime-factor counts and divisibility antichains]
 497:   \label{def:Omega-total-prime-factor-count}
 498:   For \(n\ge2\), write
 499:   \(n=\prod_p p^{a_p}\). We put
 500:   \[
 501:     \omega(n):=\#\{p:a_p>0\},
 502:     \qquad
 503:     \Omega(n):=\sum_p a_p,
 504:   \]
 505:   where \(\omega(n)\) is the number of distinct rational prime
 506:   divisors of \(n\), and \(\Omega(n)\) is the total number of prime
 507:   factors of \(n\), counted with multiplicity. We also put
 508:   \(\omega(1)=\Omega(1)=0\). Then
 509:   \[
 510:     \omega(n)\le \Omega(n)
 511:   \]
 512:   for every \(n\ge1\). A set
 513:   \(S\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\) is a divisibility antichain if no two
 514:   distinct elements \(a,b\in S\) satisfy \(a\mid b\).
 515: \end{definition}
 516: 
 517: \begin{theorem}[Coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping]
 518:   \label{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}
 519:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be recognized by an \(n\)-state
 520:   deterministic finite automaton. If \(w\in L\), \(\abs w\ge n\), and
 521:   \(\val(w)\ge2\), then there are accepted words
 522:   \[
 523:     w_0=w,\ w_1,\ w_2,\ldots
 524:   \]
 525:   with strictly increasing Zeckendorf lengths and values
 526:   \(N_i:=\val(w_i)\) such that, for every \(i\ge0\),
 527:   \[
 528:     N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}.
 529:   \]
 530:   In particular \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\) for every \(i\).
 531: \end{theorem}
 532: 
 533: \begin{proof}
 534:   Let \(\mathcal A\) be an \(n\)-state DFA recognizing \(L\). Since
 535:   \(\mathcal A\) accepts \(w\) and \(\abs w\ge n\), the usual pumping
 536:   argument gives a factorization \(w=xyz\) with \(\abs y>0\) and
 537:   \(xy^kz\in L\) for every \(k\ge0\). Put
 538:   \[
 539:     k_0=1,\qquad w_i=xy^{k_i}z,\qquad
 540:     N_i=\val(w_i).
 541:   \]
 542:   Thus \(w_0=w\) and \(N_0=\val(w)\ge2\). Suppose \(k_i\) has been
 543:   defined and \(N_i\ge2\). Let
 544:   \[
 545:     h_i=
 546:     \operatorname{ord}_{\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/N_i^2\ZZ)}(M_y)
 547:   \]
 548:   be the order of the nonempty middle-block matrix modulo \(N_i^2\),
 549:   which is defined by
 550:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}. Set
 551:   \[
 552:     k_{i+1}=k_i+h_i .
 553:   \]
 554:   Applying \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence} with
 555:   \(q=N_i^2\) gives
 556:   \[
 557:     N_{i+1}
 558:       =\val(xy^{k_{i+1}}z)
 559:       \equiv \val(xy^{k_i}z)
 560:       =N_i
 561:       \pmod {N_i^2}.
 562:   \]
 563:   Hence there is an integer \(t_i\) with
 564:   \[
 565:     N_{i+1}=N_i+N_i^2t_i=N_i(1+N_it_i).
 566:   \]
 567:   Since \(h_i\ge1\) and \(\abs y>0\), we have \(k_{i+1}>k_i\), and
 568:   the length of \(xy^{k_{i+1}}z\) is strictly larger than the length
 569:   of \(xy^{k_i}z\). Both words lie in \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), hence
 570:   are canonical Zeckendorf words. The canonical words of length \(m\)
 571:   represent exactly the interval \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\), and these
 572:   intervals are strictly ordered by length. Therefore
 573:   \(N_{i+1}>N_i\). It follows from the displayed formula that
 574:   \(t_i\ge1\).
 575: 
 576:   Put \(Q_i=1+N_it_i\). Then \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\),
 577:   \(Q_i>1\), and \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\). The last congruence gives
 578:   \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\). The strict growth also gives
 579:   \(N_{i+1}\ge N_i\ge2\), so the recursive construction continues
 580:   indefinitely. This proves the theorem.
 581: \end{proof}
 582: 
 583: \begin{theorem}[Zeckendorf divisibility-chain pumping]
 584:   \label{thm:zeckendorf-divisibility-chain-pumping}
 585:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be recognized by an \(n\)-state
 586:   deterministic finite automaton. If \(w\in L\), \(\abs w\ge n\), and
 587:   \(\val(w)\ge2\), then there are words
 588:   \[
 589:     w_0=w,\ w_1,\ w_2,\ldots
 590:   \]
 591:   in \(L\) such that the Zeckendorf lengths \(\abs{w_i}\) are strictly
 592:   increasing and
 593:   \[
 594:     \val(w_i)\ \text{strictly divides}\ \val(w_{i+1})
 595:   \]
 596:   for every \(i\ge0\).
 597: \end{theorem}
 598: 
 599: \begin{proof}
 600:   This is the immediate divisibility part of
 601:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}: there
 602:   \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\) with \(Q_i>1\), so \(N_i\) strictly divides
 603:   \(N_{i+1}\).
 604: \end{proof}
 605: 
 606: \begin{corollary}[New rational primes in every infinite regular Zeckendorf language]
 607:   \label{cor:new-rational-primes-in-every-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-language}
 608:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then \(L\)
 609:   contains accepted words \(w_0,w_1,\ldots\) such that, with
 610:   \(N_i=\val(w_i)\), the set of rational prime divisors of \(N_i\)
 611:   strictly increases at every step. More precisely, in the chain of
 612:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, every quotient
 613:   \(Q_i=N_{i+1}/N_i\) has a prime divisor that divides no earlier
 614:   \(N_j\), \(0\le j\le i\). Hence infinitely many distinct rational
 615:   primes divide values occurring in \(L\).
 616: \end{corollary}
 617: 
 618: \begin{proof}
 619:   Let an \(n\)-state DFA recognize \(L\). Since \(L\) is infinite, it
 620:   contains words of arbitrarily large length; all but at most one have
 621:   value at least \(2\). Choose \(w\in L\) with \(\abs w\ge n\) and
 622:   \(\val(w)\ge2\), and apply
 623:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. For each \(i\),
 624:   \(Q_i>1\), so choose a prime \(p_i\mid Q_i\). Since
 625:   \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\), we have \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\). Moreover
 626:   \(N_j\mid N_i\) for every \(j\le i\), because the values form a
 627:   divisibility chain. Thus \(p_i\) divides none of
 628:   \(N_0,\ldots,N_i\). But \(p_i\mid N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\), so the prime
 629:   divisor set strictly increases from \(N_i\) to \(N_{i+1}\). Iterating
 630:   gives infinitely many distinct rational prime divisors among the
 631:   accepted values.
 632: \end{proof}
 633: 
 634: \begin{corollary}[Unbounded distinct prime divisors]
 635:   \label{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}
 636:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then
 637:   \[
 638:     \sup_{w\in L}\omega(\val(w))=\infty .
 639:   \]
 640: \end{corollary}
 641: 
 642: \begin{proof}
 643:   In the sequence supplied by
 644:   \Cref{cor:new-rational-primes-in-every-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-language},
 645:   each step introduces at least one rational prime divisor not present
 646:   earlier. Hence
 647:   \[
 648:     \omega(\val(w_i))\ge \omega(\val(w_0))+i
 649:   \]
 650:   for every \(i\), and the displayed supremum is infinite.
 651: \end{proof}
 652: 
 653: \begin{corollary}[Unbounded prime-factor count in infinite regular Zeckendorf languages]
 654:   \label{cor:unbounded-prime-factor-count-in-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-languages}
 655:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then
 656:   \[
 657:     \sup_{w\in L}\Omega(\val(w))=\infty .
 658:   \]
 659: \end{corollary}
 660: 
 661: \begin{proof}
 662:   This follows from
 663:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors} and the inequality
 664:   \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\).
 665: \end{proof}
 666: 
 667: \begin{corollary}[Divisibility-antichain obstruction]
 668:   \label{cor:divisibility-antichain-obstruction}
 669:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is regular and \(\val(L)\) is a
 670:   divisibility antichain, then \(L\) is finite.
 671: \end{corollary}
 672: 
 673: \begin{proof}
 674:   If \(L\) were infinite, then it would contain an accepted word
 675:   \(w\) of length at least the number of states in a DFA for \(L\) and
 676:   with \(\val(w)\ge2\). The
 677:   divisibility-chain theorem would produce \(w_0,w_1\in L\) with
 678:   \(\val(w_0)\) strictly dividing \(\val(w_1)\), contradicting the
 679:   antichain hypothesis.
 680: \end{proof}
 681: 
 682: \begin{corollary}[Bounded-distinct-prime Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
 683:   \label{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 684:   For every fixed \(K\ge1\), the language
 685:   \[
 686:     \mathcal B_K
 687:       :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\omega(\val(w))\le K\}
 688:   \]
 689:   is infinite and contains no infinite regular sublanguage.
 690: \end{corollary}
 691: 
 692: \begin{proof}
 693:   The language \(\mathcal B_K\) is infinite because it contains the
 694:   Zeckendorf representations of all primes. If \(L\) were an infinite
 695:   regular sublanguage of \(\mathcal B_K\), then
 696:   \(\omega(\val(w))\le K\) for every \(w\in L\), contradicting
 697:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}.
 698: \end{proof}
 699: 
 700: \begin{corollary}[Finite-prime-support Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
 701:   \label{cor:finite-prime-support-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 702:   Let \(S\) be a finite set of rational primes and put
 703:   \[
 704:     \mathcal Z_S
 705:       :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:
 706:           \text{every prime divisor of }\val(w)\text{ lies in }S\}.
 707:   \]
 708:   Then \(\mathcal Z_S\) contains no infinite regular sublanguage. If
 709:   \(S\neq\emptyset\), then \(\mathcal Z_S\) is infinite, and therefore
 710:   it is REG-immune.
 711: \end{corollary}
 712: 
 713: \begin{proof}
 714:   Every value represented in \(\mathcal Z_S\) has at most \(\abs S\)
 715:   distinct prime divisors. Thus any infinite regular sublanguage of
 716:   \(\mathcal Z_S\) would contradict
 717:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}. If \(S\neq\emptyset\),
 718:   choose \(p\in S\). For every \(a\ge0\), the integer \(p^a\) has all
 719:   its prime divisors in \(S\), and its canonical Zeckendorf
 720:   representation belongs to \(\mathcal Z_S\). These values are
 721:   distinct, so \(\mathcal Z_S\) is infinite.
 722: \end{proof}
 723: 
 724: \begin{corollary}[Bounded almost-prime Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
 725:   \label{cor:bounded-almost-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 726:   For every fixed \(K\ge1\), the language
 727:   \[
 728:     \mathcal A_K
 729:       :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\Omega(\val(w))\le K\}
 730:   \]
 731:   is infinite and contains no infinite regular sublanguage.
 732: \end{corollary}
 733: 
 734: \begin{proof}
 735:   The language \(\mathcal A_K\) is infinite because it contains the
 736:   Zeckendorf representations of all primes. Since
 737:   \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\), we have
 738:   \(\mathcal A_K\subseteq\mathcal B_K\). Hence an infinite regular
 739:   sublanguage of \(\mathcal A_K\) would be an infinite regular
 740:   sublanguage of \(\mathcal B_K\), contradicting
 741:   \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}.
 742: \end{proof}
 743: 
 744: \begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf-Sch\"utzenberger regular immunity]
 745:   \label{thm:zeckendorf-schutzenberger-regular-immunity}
 746:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. If
 747:   \[
 748:     \val(L):=\{\val(w):w\in L\}
 749:   \]
 750:   is contained in the set of prime numbers, then \(L\) is finite. More
 751:   quantitatively, if an \(n\)-state deterministic finite automaton
 752:   recognizes such an \(L\), then no word of length at least \(n\) is
 753:   accepted.
 754: \end{corollary}
 755: 
 756: \begin{proof}
 757:   Since prime values have exactly one distinct prime divisor, the
 758:   finiteness assertion follows from
 759:   \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 760:   with \(K=1\). For the quantitative statement, suppose an \(n\)-state
 761:   DFA accepting such an \(L\) accepted a word \(w\) with \(\abs w\ge n\).
 762:   Then \(\val(w)\) is prime, hence at least \(2\). By
 763:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, \(L\) would contain
 764:   accepted values \(N_0\) and \(N_1=N_0Q_0\) with \(Q_0>1\), so
 765:   \(N_1\) is composite. This contradicts the assumption that
 766:   \(\val(L)\) is contained in the primes.
 767: \end{proof}
 768: 
 769: \begin{corollary}[Infinite regular Zeckendorf languages contain composites]
 770:   \label{cor:regular-zeckendorf-languages-contain-composites}
 771:   Every infinite regular language \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) contains
 772:   infinitely many words whose Zeckendorf values are composite.
 773:   Equivalently, no fixed DFA can generate infinitely many canonical
 774:   Zeckendorf primes without also generating infinitely many composite
 775:   values.
 776: \end{corollary}
 777: 
 778: \begin{proof}
 779:   By
 780:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors},
 781:   the set of values \(\omega(\val(w))\) is unbounded on \(L\). In
 782:   particular, infinitely many accepted words have
 783:   \(\omega(\val(w))\ge2\), and their values are composite.
 784: \end{proof}
 785: 
 786: \begin{corollary}[The Zeckendorf prime language is REG-immune]
 787:   \label{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-reg-immune}
 788:   The language
 789:   \[
 790:     \mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}
 791:     =\{w\in\mathcal Z:\val(w)\text{ is prime}\}
 792:   \]
 793:   is REG-immune: it is infinite and contains no infinite regular
 794:   sublanguage.
 795: \end{corollary}
 796: 
 797: \begin{proof}
 798:   Euclid's theorem and the bijection \(w\mapsto\val(w)\) from
 799:   \(\mathcal Z\) to \(\NN_{\ge1}\) show that
 800:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is infinite. If it contained an infinite

[32m succeeded in 211ms:[0m
   1: \section{Context: Zeckendorf Growth}
   2: \label{sec:zeckendorf}
   3: 
   4: This section specialises the density dichotomy to Zeckendorf words. We
   5: record only the minimal classical facts needed later---in particular the
   6: Zeckendorf growth law---and then combine them with prime-counting
   7: estimates to obtain the quantitative obstructions promised in
   8: \suppref{Section~1}.
   9: 
  10: Let
  11: \[
  12:   \mathcal Z
  13:   :=\{x_1\cdots x_m\in\{0,1\}^\ast:
  14:       x_ix_{i+1}\neq 11,\ x_m=1\}
  15: \]
  16: be the language of canonical Zeckendorf words. We write these words
  17: least-significant digit first: for \(w=x_1\cdots x_m\in\mathcal Z\),
  18: \[
  19:   \val(w):=\sum_{k=1}^m x_kF_{k+1},
  20: \]
  21: where \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=1\), \(F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n\), and
  22: \(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\). Thus \(x_m=1\) marks the most significant
  23: nonzero digit. The map \(w\mapsto\val(w)\) is a bijection from
  24: \(\mathcal Z\) onto \(\NN_{\ge 1}\) \cite{Zeckendorf1972}.
  25: Consequently \(\mathcal Z\cap\{0,1\}^{\le m}\) parametrises the
  26: interval \([1,F_{m+2})\), so words of length exactly \(m\) parametrise
  27: \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\). Equivalently,
  28: \(\abs{\mathcal Z_m}=F_m\) for every \(m\ge 1\).
  29: We write
  30: \[
  31:   \mathcal Z_m:=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\abs{w}=m\}
  32:   \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  33:   \mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
  34:   :=\{w\in\mathcal Z_m:\val(w)\text{ is prime}\},
  35: \]
  36: and set
  37: \[
  38:   \mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}
  39:   :=\bigcup_{m\ge 1}\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}.
  40: \]
  41: 
  42: \begin{lemma}[Length growth for regular Zeckendorf sublanguages]
  43:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-length-growth}
  44:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. Choose a DFA recognizing
  45:   \(L\), delete states that are not reachable from the initial state or
  46:   cannot reach an accepting state, and let \(\rho\) be the maximal
  47:   spectral radius of a strongly connected component of the resulting
  48:   automaton. Then \(\rho\le\varphi\). Moreover,
  49:   \[
  50:     \#\{w\in L:\abs w\le m\}=
  51:     \begin{cases}
  52:       \rho^{m+o(m)}, & \rho>1,\\
  53:       \exp(o(m)), & \rho\le1.
  54:     \end{cases}
  55:   \]
  56: \end{lemma}
  57: 
  58: \begin{proof}
  59:   Since \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), the number of words of length at most
  60:   \(m\) in \(L\) is bounded above by
  61:   \[
  62:     \#(\mathcal Z\cap\{0,1\}^{\le m})=F_{m+2}-1=\varphi^{m+o(m)}.
  63:   \]
  64:   For any trimmed strongly connected component \(C\) of spectral radius
  65:   \(\rho_C\), choose entry and exit paths through the trimmed automaton.
  66:   Periodic Perron--Frobenius theory gives a residue class of lengths on
  67:   which the number of paths that traverse \(C\) is
  68:   \(\rho_C^{n+o(n)}\). These paths yield distinct accepted words after
  69:   adding the fixed entry and exit words. The preceding ambient upper
  70:   bound therefore forces \(\rho_C\le\varphi\) for every component, and
  71:   hence \(\rho\le\varphi\).
  72: 
  73:   Put the trimmed adjacency matrix in block upper-triangular form by
  74:   strongly connected components. The upper bound
  75:   \(O(m^N\rho^m)\), with \(N\) depending only on the number of blocks,
  76:   follows from the usual path decomposition through this acyclic block
  77:   graph and standard finite-dimensional matrix-power estimates for the
  78:   diagonal blocks. If
  79:   \(\rho>1\), choose a reachable and co-reachable component of spectral
  80:   radius \(\rho\); the same periodic Perron--Frobenius lower bound along
  81:   one admissible residue class gives \(\rho^{m+o(m)}\) words of length at
  82:   most \(m\). If \(\rho\le1\), every non-negative integral irreducible
  83:   recurrent block has spectral radius at most \(1\), hence is either
  84:   nilpotent in the acyclic part or a permutation block on a directed
  85:   cycle. A path can pass through only finitely many such blocks, so the
  86:   total number of accepted words of length at most \(m\) is polynomial in
  87:   \(m\), and therefore \(\exp(o(m))\). This proves the displayed
  88:   estimate.
  89: \end{proof}
  90: 
  91: \begin{proposition}[Growth law for regular Zeckendorf languages]
  92:   \label{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}
  93:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. Choose a DFA recognizing
  94:   \(L\), delete states that are not reachable from the initial state or
  95:   cannot reach an accepting state, and let \(\rho\) be the maximal
  96:   spectral radius of a strongly connected component of the resulting
  97:   automaton. Define
  98:   \[
  99:     N_L(T):=\#\{w\in L:\val(w)\le T\}.
 100:   \]
 101:   If \(L=\varnothing\), then \(N_L(T)=0\) for all \(T\). Otherwise
 102:   \[
 103:     N_L(T)=T^{\alpha+o(1)},
 104:     \qquad
 105:     \alpha=
 106:     \begin{cases}
 107:       0, & \rho\le 1,\\[0.3em]
 108:       \dfrac{\log \rho}{\log \varphi}, & \rho>1.
 109:     \end{cases}
 110:     \qquad (T\to\infty).
 111:   \]
 112:   In the endpoint case \(\rho\le1\) this assertion means only
 113:   \(N_L(T)=T^{o(1)}\); no sharper polynomial asymptotic is asserted.
 114:   This is a transfer-matrix restatement for the LSD-first Zeckendorf
 115:   convention fixed at the start of the section, not a new
 116:   recognizability theorem for abstract numeration systems.
 117: \end{proposition}
 118: 
 119: \begin{proof}
 120:   We use \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-length-growth}. The general
 121:   \(S\)-recognizable-set theorem of
 122:   \cite[Theorem~7]{CharlierRampersad2011} gives the same conclusion. In
 123:   the present system the ambient count satisfies
 124:   \[
 125:     \#(\mathcal Z\cap\{0,1\}^{\le m})
 126:     =F_{m+2}-1
 127:     =\varphi^{m+o(m)},
 128:   \]
 129:   and, conversely, \(F_{m+2}=\varphi^{m+O(1)}\). Hence
 130:   \(\val(w)\le T\) is equivalent, up to an \(O(1)\) error in length, to
 131:   \(\abs w\le (\log T)/(\log\varphi)\).
 132: 
 133:   By \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-length-growth},
 134:   the number of accepted words of length at most \(m\) is
 135:   \(\rho^{m+o(m)}\) if \(\rho>1\), and \(\exp(o(m))\) if \(\rho\le1\).
 136:   Substituting \(m=(\log T)/(\log\varphi)+O(1)\) gives exactly
 137:   \(N_L(T)=T^{\alpha+o(1)}\), with \(\alpha=0\) for \(\rho\le1\) and
 138:   \(\alpha=\log\rho/\log\varphi\) for \(\rho>1\).
 139: \end{proof}
 140: 
 141: \begin{remark}\label{rem:zeckendorf-examples}
 142:   The endpoint cases and the borderline case \(\rho=1\) already occur
 143:   for simple regular sublanguages.
 144:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 145:     \item For \(L=\mathcal Z\), one has \(N_L(T)=\lfloor T\rfloor\),
 146:     \(\rho=\varphi\), and \(\alpha=1\).
 147:     \item If \(L\) is finite, then \(N_L(T)\) is eventually constant,
 148:     so \(\alpha=0\).
 149:     \item For \(L=0^\ast1\subseteq\mathcal Z\), one has \(a_m=1\) for
 150:     every \(m\), hence \(\rho=1\) and
 151:     \(N_L(T)\asymp \log T=T^{o(1)}\).
 152:   \end{enumerate}
 153: \end{remark}
 154: 
 155: \begin{remark}
 156:   The same transfer-matrix argument applies more generally to Parry
 157:   numeration systems whose admissible digit language is regular. In that
 158:   setting the regular syntax of admissible expansions replaces
 159:   \(\mathcal Z\), and the dominant exponential growth constant of the
 160:   numeration system replaces \(\varphi\); see
 161:   \cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,LecomteRigo2001,Rigo2014Vol1,Rigo2014Vol2}.
 162: \end{remark}
 163: 
 164: \begin{lemma}[Prime counts in Zeckendorf slices]
 165:   \label{lem:zeck-prime-count}
 166:   Take the explicit prime-counting constants
 167:   \(X_0,C_{\pi,1},C_{\pi,2}\) from \suppref{Lemma~1.3}. There are constants
 168:   \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z}>0\) and an explicit integer \(M_{\mathrm Z}\)
 169:   such that for all \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\),
 170:   \[
 171:     A_{\mathrm Z}\,\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
 172:     \le \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
 173:     \le B_{\mathrm Z}\,\frac{\varphi^m}{m}.
 174:   \]
 175:   Equivalently, for all sufficiently large \(m\), the prime count in
 176:   the exact Zeckendorf slice \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\) is between
 177:   \(c_1F_m/m\) and \(c_2F_m/m\) for suitable constants
 178:   \(c_1,c_2>0\).
 179: \end{lemma}
 180: 
 181: \begin{proof}
 182:   Binet's formula gives
 183:   \[
 184:     F_n=\frac{\varphi^n-(-\varphi)^{-n}}{\sqrt5}.
 185:   \]
 186:   For every \(n\ge2\),
 187:   \[
 188:     \frac{\varphi^n-1}{\sqrt5}\le F_n\le
 189:     \frac{\varphi^n+1}{\sqrt5}\le \varphi^n.
 190:   \]
 191:   Hence, for \(j=1,2\) and \(m\ge1\),
 192:   \[
 193:     c_F\varphi^m\le F_{m+j}\le C_F\varphi^m,\qquad
 194:     c_F:=\frac{\varphi^2-1}{\sqrt5\varphi},\quad
 195:     C_F:=\varphi^2.
 196:   \]
 197:   Define
 198:   \[
 199:     c_{\log}:=\frac12\log\varphi,\qquad C_{\log}:=3\log\varphi,
 200:   \]
 201:   and let \(M_{\log}\) be produced by the following finite calculation.
 202:   Increase \(M\ge2\) until, for all \(m\ge M\) and \(j=1,2\),
 203:   \[
 204:     c_{\log}m\le \log F_{m+j}\le C_{\log}m.
 205:   \]
 206:   This is reproducible from the preceding Binet bounds: the two
 207:   displayed inequalities are guaranteed once the linear inequalities
 208:   \(\log c_F+m\log\varphi\ge c_{\log}m\) and
 209:   \(\log C_F+m\log\varphi\le C_{\log}m\) hold, so it is enough to test
 210:   the resulting explicit lower bounds for \(m\).
 211: 
 212:   Choose \(M_R\ge M_{\log}\) by the following explicit Binet
 213:   majorization. No monotonicity input is needed for the exact sequence
 214:   \[
 215:     R_m
 216:     :=
 217:     \frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}\,
 218:     \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}.
 219:   \]
 220:   Write
 221:   \[
 222:     F_n=\frac{\varphi^n}{\sqrt5}(1-\varepsilon_n),
 223:     \qquad
 224:     \varepsilon_n:=(-1)^n\varphi^{-2n}.
 225:   \]
 226:   For \(m\ge1\),
 227:   \[
 228:     \frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}
 229:     =
 230:     \varphi^{-1}
 231:     \frac{1-\varepsilon_{m+1}}{1-\varepsilon_{m+2}}
 232:     \le
 233:     \varphi^{-1}
 234:     \frac{1+\varphi^{-2m-2}}{1-\varphi^{-2m-4}},
 235:   \]
 236:   and, once \(\varphi^{-2m-2}\le1/2\),
 237:   \[
 238:     \log F_{m+1}
 239:     \ge
 240:     (m+1)\log\varphi-\tfrac12\log5-2\varphi^{-2m-2}.
 241:   \]
 242:   Also \(F_{m+2}/F_{m+1}\le3\) for \(m\ge1\), so
 243:   \[
 244:     \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}
 245:     =
 246:     1+\frac{\log(F_{m+2}/F_{m+1})}{\log F_{m+1}}
 247:     \le
 248:     1+
 249:     \frac{\log3}
 250:     {(m+1)\log\varphi-\tfrac12\log5-2\varphi^{-2m-2}},
 251:   \]
 252:   whenever the denominator is positive. The two error functions
 253:   \(\varphi^{-2m-2}\) and
 254:   \(((m+1)\log\varphi-\tfrac12\log5-2\varphi^{-2m-2})^{-1}\) are bounded
 255:   above, for all \(m\ge M_{\log}\), by explicit decreasing rational
 256:   majorants obtained from rational lower and upper intervals for
 257:   \(\varphi\) and \(\log\varphi\). Consequently the product of the last
 258:   two displayed upper bounds is itself bounded by a decreasing explicit
 259:   function tending to \(\varphi^{-1}\). A finite rational search on this
 260:   majorant, using rational intervals for \(\varphi,\log\varphi,\log3\),
 261:   produces \(M_R\) such that for every \(m\ge M_R\)
 262:   \[
 263:     \frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}
 264:       \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}
 265:     \le\frac{1+\varphi^{-1}}2 .
 266:   \]
 267:   The certificate therefore verifies a single explicit Binet upper bound
 268:   that is valid for all \(m\ge M_R\), rather than a monotone-tail
 269:   assertion for \(R_m\) itself.
 270: 
 271:   Choose \(M_0\ge M_R\) by increasing \(M\) until \(F_{m+1}\ge X_0\)
 272:   and
 273:   \[
 274:     \frac12\le
 275:     \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{m\log\varphi},
 276:     \frac{\log F_{m+1}}{m\log\varphi}
 277:     \le2,
 278:   \]
 279:   for every \(m\ge M_0\). The logarithmic ratio conditions are checked
 280:   from the same linear Binet bounds, while the last displayed
 281:   product inequality is already certified by the choice of \(M_R\). Put
 282:   \[
 283:     \Delta_m:=
 284:     1-\frac{F_{m+1}}{F_{m+2}}
 285:       \frac{\log F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+1}}.
 286:   \]
 287:   Then \(\Delta_m\ge(1-\varphi^{-1})/2\) for \(m\ge M_0\). Enlarge
 288:   \(M_0\) to \(M_{\mathrm Z}\) so that, for all \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\),
 289:   \[
 290:     C_{\pi,1}\left(
 291:       \frac{F_{m+2}}{(\log F_{m+2})^2}
 292:       +\frac{F_{m+1}}{(\log F_{m+1})^2}\right)+2
 293:     \le
 294:     \frac12\,\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}\Delta_m .
 295:   \]
 296:   This last enlargement is also finite and reproducible. Using the
 297:   certified bounds \(c_F\varphi^m\le F_{m+j}\le C_F\varphi^m\) and
 298:   \(c_{\log}m\le\log F_{m+j}\le C_{\log}m\), the left-hand side is
 299:   bounded above by an explicit multiple of \(\varphi^m/m^2\) plus \(2\),
 300:   while the right-hand side is bounded below by an explicit multiple of
 301:   \(\varphi^m/m\). Once that symbolic majorization verifies the tail,
 302:   the finite prefix is checked by exact Fibonacci integers and real
 303:   interval arithmetic for the logarithms. This is the certificate for
 304:   \(M_{\mathrm Z}\).
 305:   Every word in \(\mathcal Z_m\) represents an integer in
 306:   \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\), so
 307:   \[
 308:     \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
 309:     =\pi(F_{m+2}-1)-\pi(F_{m+1}-1)
 310:   \]
 311:   and the interval-count convention following
 312:   \suppref{Lemma~1.3} gives the endpoint correction
 313:   \[
 314:     \left|
 315:       \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}-
 316:       \bigl(\pi(F_{m+2})-\pi(F_{m+1})\bigr)
 317:     \right|\le 2.
 318:   \]
 319:   Here \(\pi(x)=\#\{p\le x:p\text{ prime}\}\). For
 320:   \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\), \suppref{Lemma~1.3} gives
 321:   \[
 322:     \begin{aligned}
 323:     \pi(F_{m+2})-\pi(F_{m+1})
 324:     \ge
 325:     &\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}
 326:       -\frac{F_{m+1}}{\log F_{m+1}}\\
 327:     &{}-C_{\pi,1}\frac{F_{m+2}}{(\log F_{m+2})^2}
 328:       -C_{\pi,1}\frac{F_{m+1}}{(\log F_{m+1})^2}.
 329:     \end{aligned}
 330:   \]
 331:   By the definition of \(M_{\mathrm Z}\), using the displayed endpoint
 332:   inequality,
 333:   \[
 334:     \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
 335:     \ge \frac12\,\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}\Delta_m .
 336:   \]
 337:   The explicit Binet constants above and
 338:   \(\Delta_m\ge(1-\varphi^{-1})/2\) by construction show that one may
 339:   take
 340:   \[
 341:     A_{\mathrm Z}:=\frac{c_F(1-\varphi^{-1})}{8C_{\log}}.
 342:   \]
 343:   For the upper bound,
 344:   \[
 345:     \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}\le \pi(F_{m+2})
 346:       \le C_{\pi,2}\frac{F_{m+2}}{\log F_{m+2}}
 347:       \le \frac{C_{\pi,2}C_F}{c_{\log}}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}.
 348:   \]
 349:   Thus \(B_{\mathrm Z}:=C_{\pi,2}C_F/c_{\log}\) is admissible for every
 350:   \(m\ge M_{\mathrm Z}\).
 351: \end{proof}
 352: 
 353: All automata in this section read Zeckendorf words in the displayed
 354: LSD-first order.
 355: 
 356: \begin{lemma}[Canonical Zeckendorf presentation]
 357:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation}
 358:   The language \(\mathcal Z\) is accepted by the two-state trim
 359:   strongly connected deterministic presentation \(\mathcal N_{\mathrm Z}\)
 360:   with state set \(\{0,1\}\), initial state \(0\), accepting state
 361:   \(\{1\}\), and transitions
 362:   \[
 363:     0\xrightarrow{0}0,\qquad
 364:     0\xrightarrow{1}1,\qquad
 365:     1\xrightarrow{0}0.
 366:   \]
 367:   Its adjacency matrix is
 368:   \[
 369:     M_{\mathrm Z}=
 370:     \begin{pmatrix}
 371:       1&1\\
 372:       1&0
 373:     \end{pmatrix},
 374:   \]
 375:   its Perron root is \(\varphi\), and its graph period is \(1\). Hence
 376:   \(\mathcal N_{\mathrm Z}\) satisfies the hypotheses of
 377:   \suppref{Theorem~1.18} with \(\lambda=\varphi\), and
 378:   \(z_m=\abs{\mathcal Z_m}=F_m\).
 379: \end{lemma}
 380: 
 381: \begin{proof}
 382:   A path in the displayed graph records exactly a binary word with no
 383:   adjacent \(1\)'s: after reading a \(1\) the only available next digit
 384:   is \(0\), while from state \(0\) both digits are available. The
 385:   accepting state is \(1\), so the accepted words are precisely those
 386:   no-\(11\) words whose terminal digit is \(1\), namely
 387:   \(\mathcal Z\). The state \(0\) is initial and lies on the loop
 388:   labelled \(0\), while \(0\xrightarrow{1}1\xrightarrow{0}0\); therefore
 389:   both states are reachable from the initial state and can reach the
 390:   accepting state, and the underlying directed graph is strongly
 391:   connected. The loop at \(0\) gives period \(1\).
 392: 
 393:   The characteristic polynomial of \(M_{\mathrm Z}\) is
 394:   \(t^2-t-1\). Its spectral radius is the positive root
 395:   \(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2>1\). Finally the accepted length-\(m\) words
 396:   are the canonical Zeckendorf words of length \(m\), whose number is
 397:   \(F_m\) by the standard Fibonacci recurrence with initial values
 398:   \(F_1=F_2=1\).
 399: \end{proof}
 400: 

[32m succeeded in 244ms:[0m
 801:   regular sublanguage \(L\), then every value in \(L\) would have
 802:   exactly one distinct prime divisor, contradicting
 803:   \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 804:   with \(K=1\).
 805: \end{proof}
 806: 
 807: For a deterministic automaton \(\mathcal A\) over the Zeckendorf digit
 808: alphabet, write
 809: \[
 810:   a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)
 811:   :=\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m}.
 812: \]
 813: 
 814: \begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf symmetric-difference obstruction]
 815:   \label{cor:zeck-symmetric-diff}
 816:   All automata in this statement read the Zeckendorf words LSD-first, as
 817:   in the definition of \(\mathcal Z\) above.
 818:   Let \(\mathcal A\) be a deterministic automaton over the Zeckendorf
 819:   alphabet. Then there exists \(c_{\mathcal A,\varphi}>0\) such that
 820:   \[
 821:     \abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m)
 822:          \triangle\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
 823:     \ge c_{\mathcal A,\varphi}\,\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
 824:   \]
 825:   for all sufficiently large \(m\). If
 826:   \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)\) lies in the positive-density
 827:   alternative of \suppref{Theorem~1.18}, then along the corresponding
 828:   arithmetic progression of lengths one has the stronger bound
 829:   \(\abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m)\triangle\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
 830:   \gg \varphi^m\).
 831: \end{corollary}
 832: 
 833: \begin{proof}
 834:   Apply \suppref{Corollary~1.19} with
 835:   \[
 836:     \mathcal N=\mathcal N_{\mathrm Z},\qquad
 837:     \lambda=\varphi,\qquad
 838:     z_m=F_m,\qquad
 839:     T_m=\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}.
 840:   \]
 841:   The presentation hypotheses and \(z_m=F_m\) are supplied by
 842:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation}; the finite-budget
 843:   hypothesis
 844:   \[
 845:     A_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
 846:     \le \abs{T_m}
 847:     \le B_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
 848:   \]
 849:   is \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}. The corollary gives the all-large-\(m\)
 850:   symmetric-difference bound. Its positive-density residue-class clause
 851:   gives the stronger \(\Omega(\varphi^m)\) bound on every residue class
 852:   where the limiting relative density of
 853:   \(L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m\) inside \(\mathcal Z_m\) is positive.
 854: \end{proof}
 855: 
 856: Define recall and precision for Zeckendorf slices by
 857: \[
 858:   \mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
 859:   :=\frac{\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
 860:           {\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}},
 861:   \qquad
 862:   \mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
 863:   :=\frac{\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
 864:           {a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}
 865: \]
 866: whenever both \(\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}>0\) and
 867: \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)>0\), i.e. on lengths where the
 868: denominator is nonzero.
 869: 
 870: \begin{corollary}[No simultaneous recall and precision in Zeckendorf]
 871:   \label{cor:zeck-recall-precision}
 872:   Let \(\mathcal A\) be a deterministic automaton over the Zeckendorf
 873:   alphabet. Let \(D_Z\), the constants \(c_0,\dots,c_{D_Z-1}\), and
 874:   \(\theta\) be supplied by
 875:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation} and \suppref{Theorem~1.18}, so that
 876:   \[
 877:     \frac{a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}{F_m}
 878:     =c_{m\bmod D_Z}+O(\theta^m)
 879:   \]
 880:   for all sufficiently large \(m\). Let \(B_{\mathrm Z}\) be the upper
 881:   estimate constant from \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}. For each residue
 882:   class \(r\bmod D_Z\), exactly one of the following alternatives holds:
 883:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
 884:     \item If \(c_r=0\), then
 885:     \(\mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\) decays exponentially along
 886:     \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\).
 887:     \item If \(c_r>0\), then there is a constant
 888:     \(C_{r,\varphi}>0\), for instance
 889:     \(C_{r,\varphi}=2C_\varphi B_{\mathrm Z}/c_r\) with
 890:     \(F_m\ge C_\varphi^{-1}\varphi^m\) for all large \(m\), such that
 891:     \[
 892:       \mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\le \frac{C_{r,\varphi}}{m}
 893:     \]
 894:     for all sufficiently large \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\).
 895:   \end{enumerate}
 896:   Consequently every deterministic automaton satisfies one of the
 897:   following statements:
 898:   \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
 899:     \item \(\mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\) decays exponentially along
 900:     every residue class of lengths.
 901:     \item There exists at least one residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\) with
 902:     \(c_r>0\) such that
 903:     \(\mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\le C_{\mathcal A,\varphi}/m\) for
 904:     all sufficiently large \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\) for which precision is
 905:     defined.
 906:   \end{enumerate}
 907:   Precision is left undefined on lengths with
 908:   \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)=0\); the statement makes no uniform
 909:   precision assertion on residue classes other than those covered by
 910:   the per-residue alternative.
 911:   Consequently no fixed DFA has both recall and precision bounded below
 912:   on infinitely many Zeckendorf lengths.
 913: \end{corollary}
 914: 
 915: \begin{proof}
 916:   Fix \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z}>0\) as in
 917:   \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}; thus
 918:   \[
 919:     A_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
 920:     \le \abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
 921:     \le B_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
 922:   \]
 923:   for all sufficiently large \(m\). Also fix constants
 924:   \(c_F',C_F'>0\) such that
 925:   \(c_F'\varphi^m\le F_m\le C_F'\varphi^m\) for all large \(m\).
 926: 
 927:   Treat each residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\) separately. If \(c_r=0\), then
 928:   along \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\),
 929:   \(a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)=O(\theta^mF_m)\). Hence
 930:   \[
 931:     \mathrm{Rec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
 932:     \le
 933:     \frac{a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}
 934:          {\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
 935:     \ll m\theta^m.
 936:   \]
 937:   Since \(m\theta^m=O(\theta_r^m)\) for some \(\theta_r\in(0,1)\),
 938:   recall decays exponentially on this residue class.
 939: 
 940:   If \(c_r>0\), then for all sufficiently large \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\),
 941:   \[
 942:     a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)\ge \frac{c_r}{2}F_m
 943:     \ge \frac{c_rc_F'}{2}\varphi^m.
 944:   \]
 945:   Therefore
 946:   \[
 947:     \mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}
 948:     \le
 949:     \frac{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
 950:          {a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A)}
 951:     \le
 952:     \frac{2B_{\mathrm Z}}{c_rc_F'}\cdot\frac{1}{m}.
 953:   \]
 954:   This proves the stated per-residue alternative, with
 955:   \(C_{r,\varphi}=2B_{\mathrm Z}/(c_rc_F')\).
 956: 
 957:   If all \(c_r\) vanish, the first alternative holds on every residue
 958:   class, hence globally. Otherwise choose a residue class with
 959:   \(c_r>0\); the second alternative holds on defined lengths in that
 960:   arithmetic progression, and one may take
 961:   \(C_{\mathcal A,\varphi}=C_{r,\varphi}\).
 962: 
 963:   Finally suppose that both recall and precision were bounded below by
 964:   some \(\delta>0\) on infinitely many Zeckendorf lengths. By the
 965:   pigeonhole principle, infinitely many of those lengths lie in one
 966:   residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\). The case \(c_r=0\) contradicts the
 967:   exponential recall decay above. The case \(c_r>0\) contradicts the
 968:   bound \(\mathrm{Prec}_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\le C_{r,\varphi}/m\) along that
 969:   same residue class.
 970: \end{proof}
 971: 
 972: \begin{proposition}[Regular Zeckendorf slices exclude the prime scale]
 973:   \label{prop:zeckendorf-regular-slice-rigidity}
 974:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be a regular language, and put
 975:   \[
 976:     \ell_m:=\abs{L\cap\mathcal Z_m}.
 977:   \]
 978:   Then there are an integer \(D_Z\ge1\), non-negative constants
 979:   \(c_0,\dots,c_{D_Z-1}\), and \(\theta\in(0,1)\) such that
 980:   \[
 981:     \frac{\ell_m}{F_m}=c_{m\bmod D_Z}+O(\theta^m).
 982:   \]
 983:   Consequently, on each residue class modulo \(D_Z\), the ratio
 984:   \[
 985:     \frac{\ell_m}{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
 986:   \]
 987:   either tends to \(0\) exponentially up to the harmless polynomial
 988:   factor \(m\), or tends to \(+\infty\) at least linearly in \(m\).
 989:   In particular no regular sublanguage of \(\mathcal Z\) has
 990:   \(\ell_m=\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}\) for all sufficiently
 991:   large \(m\).
 992: \end{proposition}
 993: 
 994: \begin{proof}
 995:   Let \(\mathcal A\) be a deterministic automaton recognizing \(L\).
 996:   Since \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), one has
 997:   \[
 998:     \ell_m=\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap\mathcal Z_m}
 999:           =a_m^{(\mathrm Z)}(\mathcal A).
1000:   \]
1001:   By \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-canonical-presentation}, the canonical
1002:   Zeckendorf presentation is trim, deterministic, strongly connected,
1003:   has Perron root \(\varphi\), and satisfies
1004:   \(z_m=\abs{\mathcal Z_m}=F_m\). Applying
1005:   the background relative-density theorem
1006:   \suppref{Theorem~1.18} to this presentation and to
1007:   \(\mathcal A\) gives the displayed expansion; its proof is the
1008:   substochastic product expansion of
1009:   \suppref{Lemma~1.17} combined with the positive
1010:   periodic denominator constants from \suppref{Lemma~1.16}.
1011: 
1012:   Fix a residue class \(r\bmod D_Z\). By \Cref{lem:zeck-prime-count}
1013:   there are constants \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z}>0\) such that
1014:   \[
1015:     A_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
1016:     \le\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}
1017:     \le B_{\mathrm Z}\frac{\varphi^m}{m}
1018:   \]
1019:   for all sufficiently large \(m\). Also fix \(c_F'',C_F''>0\) such that
1020:   \(c_F''\varphi^m\le F_m\le C_F''\varphi^m\) for all large \(m\).
1021: 
1022:   If \(c_r=0\), then along \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\) we have
1023:   \[
1024:     \ell_m=O(\theta^mF_m)=O(\theta^m\varphi^m).
1025:   \]
1026:   Hence
1027:   \[
1028:     \frac{\ell_m}{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
1029:     =O(m\theta^m),
1030:   \]
1031:   which tends to \(0\) exponentially after increasing \(\theta\) within
1032:   \((0,1)\).
1033: 
1034:   If \(c_r>0\), then for all sufficiently large
1035:   \(m\equiv r\pmod {D_Z}\),
1036:   \[
1037:     \ell_m\ge\frac{c_r}{2}F_m
1038:         \ge\frac{c_rc_F''}{2}\varphi^m.
1039:   \]
1040:   Therefore
1041:   \[
1042:     \frac{\ell_m}{\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}}
1043:     \ge
1044:     \frac{c_rc_F''}{2B_{\mathrm Z}}\,m,
1045:   \]
1046:   and the ratio tends to \(+\infty\) at least linearly on this residue
1047:   class.
1048: 
1049:   Every residue class falls into exactly one of these two cases. Thus
1050:   equality
1051:   \(\ell_m=\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}}\) cannot hold for all
1052:   sufficiently large \(m\), since any fixed residue class contains
1053:   arbitrarily large lengths and on that class the ratio cannot remain
1054:   equal to \(1\).
1055: \end{proof}
1056: 
1057: \begin{corollary}[Prime Zeckendorf language is not regular]
1058:   \label{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-not-regular}
1059:   The prime Zeckendorf language \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is not
1060:   regular.
1061: \end{corollary}
1062: 
1063: This corollary is now an immediate consequence of the stronger
1064: PNT-free regular-immunity theorem. The preceding
1065: \Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-slice-rigidity} remains useful as a
1066: separate slice-scale obstruction: it explains why the exact
1067: prime-slice cardinalities cannot be reproduced by a regular
1068: sublanguage on all large lengths. General results relating
1069: \(S\)-recognizability, \(S\)-automaticity, and morphic predicates in
1070: abstract numeration systems include Maes--Rigo \cite{MaesRigo2002} and
1071: the standard references cited in \Cref{subsec:prior-work}.
1072: 
1073: \begin{proof}
1074:   This follows at once from
1075:   \Cref{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-reg-immune}, since an infinite
1076:   regular language is an infinite regular sublanguage of itself.
1077: \end{proof}
1078: 
1079: \begin{remark}[Sofic corollary]
1080:   \label{rem:zeckendorf-primes-not-sofic}
1081:   The preceding non-regularity statement excludes eventual sofic
1082:   realization of the prime Zeckendorf length slices by the following
1083:   short argument.
1084:   Let \(\mathcal B_m(X)\) denote the admissible words of length
1085:   \(m\) in a sofic shift \(X\). If
1086:   \(\mathcal B_m(X)=\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\) for all \(m\ge N\),
1087:   then
1088:   \[
1089:     \mathcal B(X)\triangle\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\subseteq \{0,1\}^{<N},
1090:   \]
1091:   a finite set. Since \(\mathcal B(X)\) is regular
1092:   (\cite[Theorem~3.2.1]{LindMarcus1995}; see also
1093:   \cite[Chapter~4]{PerrinPin2004}), this would make
1094:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) a finite symmetric difference away from a
1095:   regular language, hence regular itself, contradicting
1096:   \Cref{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-not-regular}.
1097: \end{remark}

   1: \section{Context-free pumping and Pisot recurrence actions}
   2: \label{sec:context-free-pisot}
   3: 
   4: We now pass from finite automata to pushdown automata. Recall that an
   5: infinite language is \emph{CF-immune} if it contains no infinite
   6: context-free sublanguage. Here a context-free language means one accepted
   7: by an arbitrary nondeterministic pushdown automaton, equivalently generated
   8: by a context-free grammar. We impose no determinism, unambiguity,
   9: real-time, or growth hypothesis on that automaton. The classical
  10: fixed-base theorem is due independently to Hartmanis--Shank and
  11: Sch\"utzenberger
  12: \cite{HartmanisShank1968,Schutzenberger1968}. The point below is that
  13: the square-modulus block action survives the two simultaneous pumps
  14: supplied by Ogden's lemma \cite{Ogden1968}; the interchange lemma of
  15: Ogden--Ross--Winklmann \cite{OgdenRossWinklmann1985} is a stronger
  16: related tool, but is not needed for the proof.
  17: 
  18: \begin{lemma}[Paired Zeckendorf pumping congruence]
  19:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-paired-pumping-congruence}
  20:   Let \(u,v,x,y,z\in\{0,1\}^\ast\), and put
  21:   \[
  22:     N(k):=\val(uv^kxy^kz)\qquad(k\ge0).
  23:   \]
  24:   Suppose that \(\abs v+\abs y>0\). If \(q\ge2\), and \(h\) is a
  25:   common multiple of the orders modulo \(q\) of the nonempty-block
  26:   matrices among \(M_v,M_y\) (with \(M_\varepsilon=I\)), then
  27:   \[
  28:     N(k+h)\equiv N(k)\pmod q
  29:     \qquad(k\ge0).
  30:   \]
  31: \end{lemma}
  32: 
  33: \begin{proof}
  34:   With \(v_0=(0,1,1)^t\), concatenation and
  35:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action} give
  36:   \[
  37:     N(k)=e_1^tM_zM_y^kM_xM_v^kM_uv_0.
  38:   \]
  39:   Every nonempty block matrix is invertible modulo \(q\). By the
  40:   definition of \(h\), both \(M_v^h\equiv I\pmod q\) and
  41:   \(M_y^h\equiv I\pmod q\), where the same identities are automatic for
  42:   an empty block. Substitution in the displayed product proves the
  43:   congruence.
  44: \end{proof}
  45: 
  46: \begin{theorem}[Context-free coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping]
  47:   \label{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}
  48:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be context-free. There is an integer
  49:   \(P_L\ge1\) such that every \(w\in L\) with
  50:   \(\abs w\ge P_L\) and \(\val(w)\ge2\) begins a sequence
  51:   \(w_0=w,w_1,w_2,\ldots\) in \(L\) whose lengths are strictly
  52:   increasing and whose values \(N_i=\val(w_i)\) satisfy
  53:   \[
  54:     N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad
  55:     Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i},\qquad \gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1.
  56:   \]
  57: \end{theorem}
  58: 
  59: \begin{proof}
  60:   Apply Ogden's lemma with every position distinguished. It supplies a
  61:   constant \(P_L\) such that each \(w\in L\) of length at least \(P_L\)
  62:   has a factorisation
  63:   \[
  64:     w=uvxyz,\qquad \abs v+\abs y>0,\qquad
  65:     uv^kxy^kz\in L\quad(k\ge0).
  66:   \]
  67:   The additional marked-position bound in Ogden's lemma is not needed
  68:   here. Starting with \(w_i\in L\), choose such a factorisation and
  69:   let \(h_i\) be a common multiple of the orders of \(M_v\) and \(M_y\)
  70:   in
  71:   \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/N_i^2\ZZ)\), omitting an empty block. These
  72:   orders exist by
  73:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}. Define
  74:   \[
  75:     w_{i+1}:=uv^{\,1+h_i}xy^{\,1+h_i}z.
  76:   \]
  77:   By \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-paired-pumping-congruence},
  78:   \(N_{i+1}\equiv N_i\pmod {N_i^2}\). Hence
  79:   \(N_{i+1}=N_i(1+t_iN_i)\) for an integer \(t_i\).
  80: 
  81:   Since \(h_i\ge1\) and \(\abs v+\abs y>0\), the new word is longer.
  82:   Both words belong to \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), and canonical
  83:   Zeckendorf intervals are strictly ordered by length; consequently
  84:   \(N_{i+1}>N_i\), so \(t_i\ge1\). Taking
  85:   \(Q_i=1+t_iN_i\) gives all four assertions. The new word still has
  86:   length at least \(P_L\) and value at least \(2\), so the construction
  87:   iterates.
  88: \end{proof}
  89: 
  90: \begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf CF-immunity package]
  91:   \label{cor:zeckendorf-context-free-immunity-package}
  92:   Every infinite context-free \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) satisfies
  93:   \[
  94:     \sup_{w\in L}\omega(\val(w))=\infty.
  95:   \]
  96:   Consequently, for each \(K\ge1\), the bounded-distinct-prime language
  97:   \(\mathcal B_K\), the bounded-almost-prime language \(\mathcal A_K\),
  98:   and every infinite finite-prime-support language \(\mathcal Z_S\)
  99:   are CF-immune. In particular, the Zeckendorf prime language
 100:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is CF-immune.
 101: \end{corollary}
 102: 
 103: \begin{proof}
 104:   Choose a sufficiently long word in \(L\) and apply
 105:   \Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. Since
 106:   \(N_j\mid N_i\) for \(j\le i\), while every \(Q_i>1\) is coprime to
 107:   \(N_i\), a prime divisor of \(Q_i\) divides no earlier value. Thus
 108:   \(\omega(N_i)\ge\omega(N_0)+i\). The immunity conclusions follow
 109:   exactly as in the regular cases: \(\omega\le K\) on \(\mathcal B_K\),
 110:   \(\mathcal A_K\subseteq\mathcal B_K\), and
 111:   \(\omega\le\abs S\) on \(\mathcal Z_S\). Finally prime values have
 112:   \(\omega=1\), while Euclid's theorem makes
 113:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) infinite.
 114: \end{proof}

 409: \begin{lemma}[Unimodular Zeckendorf block action]
 410:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}
 411:   After reading a prefix of length \(r\) in LSD-first order, put
 412:   \[
 413:     V_r:=
 414:     \begin{pmatrix}
 415:       N_r\\ F_{r+1}\\ F_{r+2}
 416:     \end{pmatrix},
 417:     \qquad
 418:     N_r=\sum_{j=1}^r x_jF_{j+1}.
 419:   \]
 420:   The initial vector is \(V_0=(0,F_1,F_2)^t=(0,1,1)^t\). Reading one
 421:   further digit \(\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}\) sends \(V_r\) to
 422:   \(D_\varepsilon V_r\), where
 423:   \[
 424:     D_\varepsilon:=
 425:     \begin{pmatrix}
 426:       1&0&\varepsilon\\
 427:       0&0&1\\
 428:       0&1&1
 429:     \end{pmatrix},
 430:     \qquad
 431:     \det D_\varepsilon=-1.
 432:   \]
 433:   Hence, for a word \(y=y_1\cdots y_\ell\), the block action
 434:   \[
 435:     M_y:=D_{y_\ell}\cdots D_{y_1}
 436:   \]
 437:   has determinant \((-1)^\ell\). In particular, if \(\ell>0\), then
 438:   the reduction of \(M_y\) belongs to
 439:   \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\) for every \(q\ge2\).
 440: \end{lemma}
 441: 
 442: \begin{proof}
 443:   If the next digit is \(\varepsilon\), then it occupies position
 444:   \(r+1\) and contributes \(\varepsilon F_{r+2}\) to the value. Thus
 445:   \[
 446:     N_{r+1}=N_r+\varepsilon F_{r+2},\qquad
 447:     F_{r+2}=F_{r+2},\qquad
 448:     F_{r+3}=F_{r+1}+F_{r+2}.
 449:   \]
 450:   These three identities are exactly
 451:   \(V_{r+1}=D_\varepsilon V_r\). Expanding the determinant along the
 452:   first row gives
 453:   \[
 454:     \det D_\varepsilon
 455:     =\det\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}
 456:      +\varepsilon\det\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}
 457:     =-1.
 458:   \]
 459:   Multiplicativity of the determinant gives
 460:   \(\det M_y=(-1)^\ell\). Since \(\pm1\) is a unit modulo every
 461:   \(q\ge2\), the reduction of \(M_y\) is invertible over
 462:   \(\ZZ/q\ZZ\).
 463: \end{proof}
 464: 
 465: \begin{lemma}[Zeckendorf pumping congruence]
 466:   \label{lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence}
 467:   Let \(x,y,z\in\{0,1\}^\ast\) with \(\abs y>0\), and put
 468:   \[
 469:     N(k):=\val(xy^kz)
 470:   \]
 471:   whenever the displayed word is read in LSD-first order. If \(q\ge2\)
 472:   and \(h\) is the order of \(M_y\) in
 473:   \(\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/q\ZZ)\), then
 474:   \[
 475:     N(k+h)\equiv N(k)\pmod q
 476:   \]
 477:   for every \(k\ge0\). In particular, if \(p=N(k)\) is prime, then
 478:   \(N(k+h)\) is divisible by \(p\).
 479: \end{lemma}
 480: 
 481: \begin{proof}
 482:   Let \(v_0=(0,1,1)^t\). By
 483:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}, concatenation of
 484:   LSD-first blocks gives
 485:   \[
 486:     N(k)=
 487:     e_1^t\,M_zM_y^kM_xv_0,
 488:   \]
 489:   where \(e_1=(1,0,0)^t\). Since \(M_y^h\equiv I\pmod q\), we have
 490:   \(M_y^{k+h}\equiv M_y^k\pmod q\). Multiplying on the left by
 491:   \(M_z\), on the right by \(M_xv_0\), and taking the first coordinate
 492:   gives the congruence for \(N(k+h)\) and \(N(k)\). If \(q=p=N(k)\) is
 493:   prime, this congruence says \(N(k+h)\equiv0\pmod p\).
 494: \end{proof}
 495: 
 496: \begin{definition}[Prime-factor counts and divisibility antichains]
 497:   \label{def:Omega-total-prime-factor-count}
 498:   For \(n\ge2\), write
 499:   \(n=\prod_p p^{a_p}\). We put
 500:   \[
 501:     \omega(n):=\#\{p:a_p>0\},
 502:     \qquad
 503:     \Omega(n):=\sum_p a_p,
 504:   \]
 505:   where \(\omega(n)\) is the number of distinct rational prime
 506:   divisors of \(n\), and \(\Omega(n)\) is the total number of prime
 507:   factors of \(n\), counted with multiplicity. We also put
 508:   \(\omega(1)=\Omega(1)=0\). Then
 509:   \[
 510:     \omega(n)\le \Omega(n)
 511:   \]
 512:   for every \(n\ge1\). A set
 513:   \(S\subseteq\NN_{\ge1}\) is a divisibility antichain if no two
 514:   distinct elements \(a,b\in S\) satisfy \(a\mid b\).
 515: \end{definition}
 516: 
 517: \begin{theorem}[Coprime-quotient Zeckendorf pumping]
 518:   \label{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}
 519:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be recognized by an \(n\)-state
 520:   deterministic finite automaton. If \(w\in L\), \(\abs w\ge n\), and
 521:   \(\val(w)\ge2\), then there are accepted words
 522:   \[
 523:     w_0=w,\ w_1,\ w_2,\ldots
 524:   \]
 525:   with strictly increasing Zeckendorf lengths and values
 526:   \(N_i:=\val(w_i)\) such that, for every \(i\ge0\),
 527:   \[
 528:     N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}.
 529:   \]
 530:   In particular \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\) for every \(i\).
 531: \end{theorem}
 532: 
 533: \begin{proof}
 534:   Let \(\mathcal A\) be an \(n\)-state DFA recognizing \(L\). Since
 535:   \(\mathcal A\) accepts \(w\) and \(\abs w\ge n\), the usual pumping
 536:   argument gives a factorization \(w=xyz\) with \(\abs y>0\) and
 537:   \(xy^kz\in L\) for every \(k\ge0\). Put
 538:   \[
 539:     k_0=1,\qquad w_i=xy^{k_i}z,\qquad
 540:     N_i=\val(w_i).
 541:   \]
 542:   Thus \(w_0=w\) and \(N_0=\val(w)\ge2\). Suppose \(k_i\) has been
 543:   defined and \(N_i\ge2\). Let
 544:   \[
 545:     h_i=
 546:     \operatorname{ord}_{\operatorname{GL}_3(\ZZ/N_i^2\ZZ)}(M_y)
 547:   \]
 548:   be the order of the nonempty middle-block matrix modulo \(N_i^2\),
 549:   which is defined by
 550:   \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-unimodular-block-action}. Set
 551:   \[
 552:     k_{i+1}=k_i+h_i .
 553:   \]
 554:   Applying \Cref{lem:zeckendorf-pumping-congruence} with
 555:   \(q=N_i^2\) gives
 556:   \[
 557:     N_{i+1}
 558:       =\val(xy^{k_{i+1}}z)
 559:       \equiv \val(xy^{k_i}z)
 560:       =N_i
 561:       \pmod {N_i^2}.
 562:   \]
 563:   Hence there is an integer \(t_i\) with
 564:   \[
 565:     N_{i+1}=N_i+N_i^2t_i=N_i(1+N_it_i).
 566:   \]
 567:   Since \(h_i\ge1\) and \(\abs y>0\), we have \(k_{i+1}>k_i\), and
 568:   the length of \(xy^{k_{i+1}}z\) is strictly larger than the length
 569:   of \(xy^{k_i}z\). Both words lie in \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\), hence
 570:   are canonical Zeckendorf words. The canonical words of length \(m\)
 571:   represent exactly the interval \([F_{m+1},F_{m+2})\), and these
 572:   intervals are strictly ordered by length. Therefore
 573:   \(N_{i+1}>N_i\). It follows from the displayed formula that
 574:   \(t_i\ge1\).
 575: 
 576:   Put \(Q_i=1+N_it_i\). Then \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\),
 577:   \(Q_i>1\), and \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\). The last congruence gives
 578:   \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\). The strict growth also gives
 579:   \(N_{i+1}\ge N_i\ge2\), so the recursive construction continues
 580:   indefinitely. This proves the theorem.
 581: \end{proof}
 582: 
 583: \begin{theorem}[Zeckendorf divisibility-chain pumping]
 584:   \label{thm:zeckendorf-divisibility-chain-pumping}
 585:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be recognized by an \(n\)-state
 586:   deterministic finite automaton. If \(w\in L\), \(\abs w\ge n\), and
 587:   \(\val(w)\ge2\), then there are words
 588:   \[
 589:     w_0=w,\ w_1,\ w_2,\ldots
 590:   \]
 591:   in \(L\) such that the Zeckendorf lengths \(\abs{w_i}\) are strictly
 592:   increasing and
 593:   \[
 594:     \val(w_i)\ \text{strictly divides}\ \val(w_{i+1})
 595:   \]
 596:   for every \(i\ge0\).
 597: \end{theorem}
 598: 
 599: \begin{proof}
 600:   This is the immediate divisibility part of
 601:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}: there
 602:   \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\) with \(Q_i>1\), so \(N_i\) strictly divides
 603:   \(N_{i+1}\).
 604: \end{proof}
 605: 
 606: \begin{corollary}[New rational primes in every infinite regular Zeckendorf language]
 607:   \label{cor:new-rational-primes-in-every-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-language}
 608:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then \(L\)
 609:   contains accepted words \(w_0,w_1,\ldots\) such that, with
 610:   \(N_i=\val(w_i)\), the set of rational prime divisors of \(N_i\)
 611:   strictly increases at every step. More precisely, in the chain of
 612:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, every quotient
 613:   \(Q_i=N_{i+1}/N_i\) has a prime divisor that divides no earlier
 614:   \(N_j\), \(0\le j\le i\). Hence infinitely many distinct rational
 615:   primes divide values occurring in \(L\).
 616: \end{corollary}
 617: 
 618: \begin{proof}
 619:   Let an \(n\)-state DFA recognize \(L\). Since \(L\) is infinite, it
 620:   contains words of arbitrarily large length; all but at most one have

[32m succeeded in 179ms:[0m
 621:   value at least \(2\). Choose \(w\in L\) with \(\abs w\ge n\) and
 622:   \(\val(w)\ge2\), and apply
 623:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}. For each \(i\),
 624:   \(Q_i>1\), so choose a prime \(p_i\mid Q_i\). Since
 625:   \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\), we have \(\gcd(Q_i,N_i)=1\). Moreover
 626:   \(N_j\mid N_i\) for every \(j\le i\), because the values form a
 627:   divisibility chain. Thus \(p_i\) divides none of
 628:   \(N_0,\ldots,N_i\). But \(p_i\mid N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\), so the prime
 629:   divisor set strictly increases from \(N_i\) to \(N_{i+1}\). Iterating
 630:   gives infinitely many distinct rational prime divisors among the
 631:   accepted values.
 632: \end{proof}
 633: 
 634: \begin{corollary}[Unbounded distinct prime divisors]
 635:   \label{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}
 636:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then
 637:   \[
 638:     \sup_{w\in L}\omega(\val(w))=\infty .
 639:   \]
 640: \end{corollary}
 641: 
 642: \begin{proof}
 643:   In the sequence supplied by
 644:   \Cref{cor:new-rational-primes-in-every-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-language},
 645:   each step introduces at least one rational prime divisor not present
 646:   earlier. Hence
 647:   \[
 648:     \omega(\val(w_i))\ge \omega(\val(w_0))+i
 649:   \]
 650:   for every \(i\), and the displayed supremum is infinite.
 651: \end{proof}
 652: 
 653: \begin{corollary}[Unbounded prime-factor count in infinite regular Zeckendorf languages]
 654:   \label{cor:unbounded-prime-factor-count-in-infinite-regular-zeckendorf-languages}
 655:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is infinite and regular, then
 656:   \[
 657:     \sup_{w\in L}\Omega(\val(w))=\infty .
 658:   \]
 659: \end{corollary}
 660: 
 661: \begin{proof}
 662:   This follows from
 663:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors} and the inequality
 664:   \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\).
 665: \end{proof}
 666: 
 667: \begin{corollary}[Divisibility-antichain obstruction]
 668:   \label{cor:divisibility-antichain-obstruction}
 669:   If \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) is regular and \(\val(L)\) is a
 670:   divisibility antichain, then \(L\) is finite.
 671: \end{corollary}
 672: 
 673: \begin{proof}
 674:   If \(L\) were infinite, then it would contain an accepted word
 675:   \(w\) of length at least the number of states in a DFA for \(L\) and
 676:   with \(\val(w)\ge2\). The
 677:   divisibility-chain theorem would produce \(w_0,w_1\in L\) with
 678:   \(\val(w_0)\) strictly dividing \(\val(w_1)\), contradicting the
 679:   antichain hypothesis.
 680: \end{proof}
 681: 
 682: \begin{corollary}[Bounded-distinct-prime Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
 683:   \label{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 684:   For every fixed \(K\ge1\), the language
 685:   \[
 686:     \mathcal B_K
 687:       :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\omega(\val(w))\le K\}
 688:   \]
 689:   is infinite and contains no infinite regular sublanguage.
 690: \end{corollary}
 691: 
 692: \begin{proof}
 693:   The language \(\mathcal B_K\) is infinite because it contains the
 694:   Zeckendorf representations of all primes. If \(L\) were an infinite
 695:   regular sublanguage of \(\mathcal B_K\), then
 696:   \(\omega(\val(w))\le K\) for every \(w\in L\), contradicting
 697:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}.
 698: \end{proof}
 699: 
 700: \begin{corollary}[Finite-prime-support Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
 701:   \label{cor:finite-prime-support-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 702:   Let \(S\) be a finite set of rational primes and put
 703:   \[
 704:     \mathcal Z_S
 705:       :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:
 706:           \text{every prime divisor of }\val(w)\text{ lies in }S\}.
 707:   \]
 708:   Then \(\mathcal Z_S\) contains no infinite regular sublanguage. If
 709:   \(S\neq\emptyset\), then \(\mathcal Z_S\) is infinite, and therefore
 710:   it is REG-immune.
 711: \end{corollary}
 712: 
 713: \begin{proof}
 714:   Every value represented in \(\mathcal Z_S\) has at most \(\abs S\)
 715:   distinct prime divisors. Thus any infinite regular sublanguage of
 716:   \(\mathcal Z_S\) would contradict
 717:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors}. If \(S\neq\emptyset\),
 718:   choose \(p\in S\). For every \(a\ge0\), the integer \(p^a\) has all
 719:   its prime divisors in \(S\), and its canonical Zeckendorf
 720:   representation belongs to \(\mathcal Z_S\). These values are
 721:   distinct, so \(\mathcal Z_S\) is infinite.
 722: \end{proof}
 723: 
 724: \begin{corollary}[Bounded almost-prime Zeckendorf languages are REG-immune]
 725:   \label{cor:bounded-almost-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 726:   For every fixed \(K\ge1\), the language
 727:   \[
 728:     \mathcal A_K
 729:       :=\{w\in\mathcal Z:\Omega(\val(w))\le K\}
 730:   \]
 731:   is infinite and contains no infinite regular sublanguage.
 732: \end{corollary}
 733: 
 734: \begin{proof}
 735:   The language \(\mathcal A_K\) is infinite because it contains the
 736:   Zeckendorf representations of all primes. Since
 737:   \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\), we have
 738:   \(\mathcal A_K\subseteq\mathcal B_K\). Hence an infinite regular
 739:   sublanguage of \(\mathcal A_K\) would be an infinite regular
 740:   sublanguage of \(\mathcal B_K\), contradicting
 741:   \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}.
 742: \end{proof}
 743: 
 744: \begin{corollary}[Zeckendorf-Sch\"utzenberger regular immunity]
 745:   \label{thm:zeckendorf-schutzenberger-regular-immunity}
 746:   Let \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) be regular. If
 747:   \[
 748:     \val(L):=\{\val(w):w\in L\}
 749:   \]
 750:   is contained in the set of prime numbers, then \(L\) is finite. More
 751:   quantitatively, if an \(n\)-state deterministic finite automaton
 752:   recognizes such an \(L\), then no word of length at least \(n\) is
 753:   accepted.
 754: \end{corollary}
 755: 
 756: \begin{proof}
 757:   Since prime values have exactly one distinct prime divisor, the
 758:   finiteness assertion follows from
 759:   \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 760:   with \(K=1\). For the quantitative statement, suppose an \(n\)-state
 761:   DFA accepting such an \(L\) accepted a word \(w\) with \(\abs w\ge n\).
 762:   Then \(\val(w)\) is prime, hence at least \(2\). By
 763:   \Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping}, \(L\) would contain
 764:   accepted values \(N_0\) and \(N_1=N_0Q_0\) with \(Q_0>1\), so
 765:   \(N_1\) is composite. This contradicts the assumption that
 766:   \(\val(L)\) is contained in the primes.
 767: \end{proof}
 768: 
 769: \begin{corollary}[Infinite regular Zeckendorf languages contain composites]
 770:   \label{cor:regular-zeckendorf-languages-contain-composites}
 771:   Every infinite regular language \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\) contains
 772:   infinitely many words whose Zeckendorf values are composite.
 773:   Equivalently, no fixed DFA can generate infinitely many canonical
 774:   Zeckendorf primes without also generating infinitely many composite
 775:   values.
 776: \end{corollary}
 777: 
 778: \begin{proof}
 779:   By
 780:   \Cref{cor:unbounded-distinct-prime-divisors},
 781:   the set of values \(\omega(\val(w))\) is unbounded on \(L\). In
 782:   particular, infinitely many accepted words have
 783:   \(\omega(\val(w))\ge2\), and their values are composite.
 784: \end{proof}
 785: 
 786: \begin{corollary}[The Zeckendorf prime language is REG-immune]
 787:   \label{cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-reg-immune}
 788:   The language
 789:   \[
 790:     \mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}
 791:     =\{w\in\mathcal Z:\val(w)\text{ is prime}\}
 792:   \]
 793:   is REG-immune: it is infinite and contains no infinite regular
 794:   sublanguage.
 795: \end{corollary}
 796: 
 797: \begin{proof}
 798:   Euclid's theorem and the bijection \(w\mapsto\val(w)\) from
 799:   \(\mathcal Z\) to \(\NN_{\ge1}\) show that
 800:   \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) is infinite. If it contained an infinite
 801:   regular sublanguage \(L\), then every value in \(L\) would have
 802:   exactly one distinct prime divisor, contradicting
 803:   \Cref{cor:bounded-distinct-prime-zeckendorf-languages-reg-immune}
 804:   with \(K=1\).
 805: \end{proof}

 468: def construct_divisibility_tree(
 469:     root: int,
 470:     nodes: Sequence[tuple[int, ...]],
 471:     thresholds: dict[tuple[int, ...], int],
 472: ) -> tuple[dict[tuple[int, ...], int], dict[tuple[int, ...], int]]:
 473:     if not nodes or nodes[0] != () or root < 1:
 474:         raise ValueError("nodes must begin with the root and the root value must be positive")
 475:     values = {(): root}
 476:     edge_quotients: dict[tuple[int, ...], int] = {}
 477:     for node in nodes[1:]:
 478:         parent = node[:-1]
 479:         if parent not in values:
 480:             raise ValueError("every parent must precede its children")
 481:         threshold = thresholds[node]
 482:         small_prime_product = 1
 483:         for candidate in range(2, threshold + 1):
 484:             if is_prime(candidate):
 485:                 small_prime_product *= candidate
 486:         previous_product = root * small_prime_product
 487:         for quotient in edge_quotients.values():
 488:             previous_product *= quotient
 489:         parent_value = values[parent]
 490:         quotient = 1 + parent_value * previous_product
 491:         values[node] = parent_value * quotient
 492:         edge_quotients[node] = quotient
 493:     return values, edge_quotients
 494: 
 495: 
 496: def verify_pump_witness(
 497:     system: LinearSystem,
 498:     word: Word,
 499:     cuts: tuple[int, int, int, int],
 500:     require_canonical: bool = False,
 501: ) -> PumpWitness:
 502:     original = value(system, word)
 503:     if not is_prime(original):
 504:         raise ValueError("the original word must represent a prime")
 505:     if original and system.trailing_coefficient % original == 0:
 506:         raise NonInvertibleAction("the represented prime divides the trailing coefficient")
 507:     a, b, c, d = cuts
 508:     v, y = word[a:b], word[c:d]
 509:     orders = [
 510:         matrix_order(block_matrix(system, block), original * original)
 511:         for block in (v, y)
 512:         if block
 513:     ]
 514:     return_time = lcm(*orders)
 515:     exponent = 1 + return_time
 516:     result_word = pumped_word(word, cuts, exponent)
 517:     result_value = value(system, result_word)
 518:     before = is_canonical(system, word)
 519:     after = is_canonical(system, result_word)
 520:     if require_canonical and not (before and after):
 521:         raise AssertionError("the selected pumping does not preserve canonical syntax")
 522:     if result_value % (original * original) != original:
 523:         raise AssertionError("square-modulus pumping congruence failed")
 524:     return PumpWitness(
 525:         system=system.name,
 526:         original_word="".join(map(str, word)),
 527:         original_value=original,
 528:         cuts=cuts,
 529:         return_time=return_time,
 530:         pump_exponent=exponent,
 531:         pumped_length=len(result_word),
 532:         pumped_value=result_value,
 533:         quotient_mod_original=(result_value // original) % original,
 534:         canonical_before=before,
 535:         canonical_after=after,
 536:     )
 537: 
 538: 
 539: def check_affine_action(system: LinearSystem, max_length: int = 5) -> int:
 540:     cases = 0
 541:     initial_state = (0,) + system.initials
 542:     for length in range(max_length + 1):
 543:         for word in product(range(system.max_digit + 1), repeat=length):
 544:             transformed = matvec(block_matrix(system, word), initial_state)
 545:             expected_weights = weights(system, length + system.dimension)[length:]
 546:             expected = (value(system, word),) + tuple(expected_weights)
 547:             if transformed != expected:
 548:                 raise AssertionError(
 549:                     f"affine action mismatch for {system.name}, word={word}: "
 550:                     f"{transformed} != {expected}"
 551:                 )
 552:             cases += 1
 553:     return cases
 554:

 556: def search_singular_counterexample() -> dict[str, int | str | bool]:
 557:     system = system_by_name("quadratic_nonunit")
 558:     matrix = block_matrix(system, (0,))
 559:     modulus = 2

 563:         "modulus": modulus,
 564:         "trailing_coefficient": system.trailing_coefficient,
 565:         "block": "0",
 566:         "determinant": determinant(matrix),
 567:         "invertible": gcd(determinant(matrix), modulus) == 1,
