 571: def verify_inflated_fibonacci_separation(
 572:     primes: Sequence[int] = (2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13),
 573:     maximum_power: int = 4,
 574:     maximum_index: int = 24,
 575: ) -> dict[str, int]:
 576:     """Check the ambient/reachable determinant separation for Fibonacci tails."""
 577:     fibonacci = [1, 2]
 578:     while len(fibonacci) <= maximum_index + 3:
 579:         fibonacci.append(fibonacci[-1] + fibonacci[-2])
 580: 
 581:     reachable_state = (1, 2, 3)
 582:     reachable_next = (2, 3, 5)
 583:     reachable_action = ((0, 1), (1, 1))
 584:     reachable_determinant = determinant(reachable_action)
 585:     saturation_minors = (
 586:         reachable_state[0] * reachable_next[1]
 587:         - reachable_state[1] * reachable_next[0],
 588:         reachable_state[0] * reachable_next[2]
 589:         - reachable_state[2] * reachable_next[0],
 590:         reachable_state[1] * reachable_next[2]
 591:         - reachable_state[2] * reachable_next[1],
 592:     )
 593: 
 594:     cases = 0
 595:     failures = 0
 596: 
 597:     def check(condition: bool) -> None:
 598:         nonlocal cases, failures
 599:         cases += 1
 600:         failures += int(not condition)
 601: 
 602:     check(gcd(*saturation_minors) == 1)
 603:     check(reachable_determinant == -1)
 604: 
 605:     for prime in primes:
 606:         inflated = LinearSystem(
 607:             name=f"inflated_fibonacci_{prime}",
 608:             polynomial=f"(x-{prime})(x^2-x-1)",
 609:             recurrence=(-prime, 1 - prime, prime + 1),
 610:             initials=reachable_state,
 611:             max_digit=1,
 612:         )
 613:         companion = tuple(
 614:             tuple(row[1:]) for row in digit_matrix(inflated, 0)[1:]
 615:         )
 616:         check(determinant(companion) == -prime)
 617:         check(matvec(companion, reachable_state) == reachable_next)
 618:         twice = matvec(companion, reachable_next)
 619:         check(
 620:             tuple(
 621:                 twice[index] - reachable_next[index] - reachable_state[index]
 622:                 for index in range(3)
 623:             )
 624:             == (0, 0, 0)
 625:         )
 626: 
 627:         for index in range(maximum_index + 1):
 628:             check(
 629:                 fibonacci[index + 3]
 630:                 == -prime * fibonacci[index]
 631:                 + (1 - prime) * fibonacci[index + 1]
 632:                 + (prime + 1) * fibonacci[index + 2]
 633:             )
 634: 
 635:         for power in range(1, maximum_power + 1):
 636:             modulus = prime**power
 637:             check(gcd(determinant(companion), modulus) != 1)
 638:             check(gcd(reachable_determinant, modulus) == 1)
 639:             state = tuple(entry % modulus for entry in reachable_state)
 640:             for _ in range(maximum_index + 1):
 641:                 following = tuple(entry % modulus for entry in matvec(companion, state))
 642:                 twice = tuple(entry % modulus for entry in matvec(companion, following))
 643:                 check(
 644:                     all(
 645:                         (twice[index] - following[index] - state[index]) % modulus
 646:                         == 0
 647:                         for index in range(3)
 648:                     )
 649:                 )
 650:                 check(any(entry % modulus for entry in state))
 651:                 state = following
 652: 
 653:     return {
 654:         "cases": cases,
 655:         "failures": failures,
 656:         "reachable_rank": 2,
 657:         "reachable_determinant": reachable_determinant,
 658:     }

# Split-out manuscript

This folder contains the standalone syntax-focused manuscript split out
from
`2026_dynamical_zeta_finite_part_spectral_fingerprint_etds`.

Focus of this paper:

- density dichotomies for deterministic finite automata,
- lower bounds against finite-state approximation of binary primes,
- regular and sofic obstructions in Zeckendorf syntax,
- analytic obstructions for prime-supported Euler products and finite
  determinants.

The exposition has been rewritten as a single finite-state obstruction
paper rather than a miscellaneous section of the larger draft.

## Theorem Inventory

### Block A: Probabilistic (DFA density)

- `thm:dfa-density-dichotomy`: Accepted-word density is eventually periodic mod p up to O(theta^m); either exponentially sparse or positive-density along a residue class
- `cor:dfa-prime-symmetric-diff`: Every DFA language has symmetric difference >= c 2^m/m with binary prime language
- `cor:dfa-prime-recall-precision`: No fixed DFA achieves both recall and precision bounded away from 0 on binary primes

### Block B: Combinatorial (Zeckendorf)

- `thm:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw`: Regular sublanguages of Zeckendorf language have counting function T^{alpha+o(1)}
- `cor:zeckendorf-prime-language-not-regular`: Language of Zeckendorf representations of primes is not regular
- `prop:sofic-counts-exponential-polynomial`: Sofic shift word counts are exponential polynomials (cited-standard, Lind-Marcus)
- `thm:zeckendorf-primes-not-sofic`: Length slices of prime Zeckendorf language cannot be admissible words of any sofic shift

### Block C: Analytic

- `thm:euler-product-natural-boundary`: Euler product with infinitely many prime-indexed positive factors has |z|=1 as natural boundary
- `prop:finite-zeta-imaginary-periodicity`: det(I - e^{-s}M)^{-1} is 2pi i-periodic; Riemann zeta is not

### Dependency chain

```
PNT (external) --+---> Cor 2 ---> Cor 3

PNT (external) --+---> Thm 7
Prop 6 (cited) ---+
Thm 8: self-contained (density of roots of unity)
Prop 9: self-contained (periodicity of exponential)
```

**Total:** 9 body-level claims. All proved (Prop 6 cited-standard).

## Stage Notes

### P2 Research Extension

**Assessment:** Results range from folklore to mild extensions of standard material. The value proposition is systematic assembly across three mathematical perspectives (automata theory, combinatorics on words, analytic number theory), not any single deep theorem.

**Novelty by result:**
- Thm 1 (density dichotomy): Folklore in automata theory (finite Markov chain consequence)
- Cor 2-3 (binary primes): Explicit formulations appear new; underlying fact is implicit in automatic-sequences literature
- Thm 4 (Zeckendorf regular power law): Standard via numeration-system theory (Frougny, Bruyere-Hansel)
- Cor 5 (prime Zeckendorf not regular): Folklore consequence of PNT + growth theory
- Prop 6 (sofic counts): Textbook (Lind-Marcus)
- Thm 7 (primes not sofic): Straightforward application of exponential-polynomial structure
- Thm 8 (natural boundary): Classical technique (Estermann 1928); specific Euler-product formulation may be new
- Prop 9 (periodicity rigidity): Elementary; clean formulation

**Scope decisions:** Paper restricts to complete DFA, binary alphabet, canonical Zeckendorf, non-negative Euler exponents. These restrictions simplify proofs but limit generality. Zeckendorf argument extends to all Parry numeration systems at no extra cost -- should be stated.

**Gaps identified:**
1. DFA definition does not address leading-zeros convention for binary numbers
2. PNT application uses asymptotic notation without specifying implied constants
3. Thm 4 proof: lambda=0 case (finite language) needs separate handling
4. Thm 8 non-cancellation argument too terse; needs explicit clarification or logarithmic approach
5. Prop 9 implicitly requires irrationality of log 2
6. No computational/reproducibility component (purely theoretical)

**Missing references:** Allouche-Shallit (2003), Cobham (1972), Mauduit-Rivat (2010), Bruyere-Hansel (1997), Hardy-Wright, Estermann (1928), Flajolet-Sedgewick (2009), Frougny (1992).

1276: \subsection{Relative density for deterministic presentations}
1277: 
1278: We use the following deterministic presentation convention in the
1279: relative-density statements.
1280: 
1281: \begin{definition}[Trim deterministic presentation]
1282:   \label{def:trim-deterministic-presentation}
1283:   A deterministic presentation over a finite alphabet \(\Sigma\) is a
1284:   tuple
1285:   \[
1286:     \mathcal N=(Q_N,\Sigma,\delta_N,q_N,F_N),
1287:   \]
1288:   where \(Q_N\) is finite, \(q_N\in Q_N\), \(F_N\subseteq Q_N\), and
1289:   \(\delta_N:Q_N\times\Sigma\rightharpoonup Q_N\) is a partial
1290:   transition map. A word is accepted if it labels a path from \(q_N\)
1291:   to a state in \(F_N\). Its adjacency matrix is
1292:   \[
1293:     (B_N)_{qq'}
1294:     :=\#\{a\in\Sigma:\delta_N(q,a)=q'\};
1295:   \]
1296:   thus several letters leading from \(q\) to the same state \(q'\) are
1297:   counted with their multiplicity, while unavailable letters contribute
1298:   no edge. The presentation is trim if every state is reachable from
1299:   \(q_N\) and can reach some state of \(F_N\). It is strongly connected
1300:   if the directed graph with edges \(q\to q'\) whenever
1301:   \(\delta_N(q,a)=q'\) for some \(a\in\Sigma\) is strongly connected.
1302: 
1303:   If \(\mathcal A=(Q_A,\Sigma,\delta_A,q_A,F_A)\) is another
1304:   deterministic automaton over the same alphabet, its synchronous
1305:   product with \(\mathcal N\) has state set \(Q_N\times Q_A\), initial
1306:   state \((q_N,q_A)\), accepting set \(F_N\times F_A\), and the partial
1307:   transition
1308:   \[
1309:     (q,s)\xrightarrow{a}(\delta_N(q,a),\delta_A(s,a))
1310:   \]
1311:   exactly for letters \(a\) for which both displayed transitions are
1312:   defined. Completing \(\mathcal A\) by a sink state, when desired, does
1313:   not change the intersection language.
1314: \end{definition}
1315: 
1316: We first record the finite-budget statement in the general
1317: multicomponent form. This is the version needed when the deterministic
1318: presentation is trim but its adjacency matrix has several maximal
1319: strongly connected components, possibly connected by transient paths.
1320: The polynomial factors in the statement are exactly the factors absent
1321: from the strongly connected specialization below.
1322: 
1323: \noindent\textit{\(\lambda\)-active residues.}
1324: Let \(\mathcal N\) be a trim deterministic presentation with adjacency
1325: matrix \(B_N\), let \(\lambda=\rho(B_N)>1\), and let
1326: \(z_m=\abs{L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m}\). After a period \(D\) has been
1327: fixed, call a residue class \(r\bmod D\) \(\lambda\)-active if
1328: \[
1329:   z_m\not=O(\theta^m)
1330:   \qquad (m\equiv r\pmod D)
1331: \]
1332: for every \(\theta<\lambda\). Equivalently, accepted words in that
1333: residue class have exponential growth rate \(\lambda\). A residue class
1334: can contain infinitely many accepted lengths without being
1335: \(\lambda\)-active: in a reducible presentation those words may be
1336: supplied only by components of smaller spectral radius. The
1337: multicomponent theorem below separates this lower-growth phenomenon
1338: from the Perron-scale comparison.
1339: 
1340: \begin{theorem}[Multicomponent finite-budget obstruction]
1341:   \label{thm:multicomponent-finite-budget}
1342:   Let \(\mathcal N\) be a trim deterministic presentation,
1343:   not necessarily strongly connected, with adjacency matrix \(B_N\).
1344:   Let \(\lambda:=\rho(B_N)>1\), and put
1345:   \(z_m=\abs{L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m}\). Then there are an integer
1346:   period \(D\ge1\), a number \(0<\theta<\lambda\), an integer
1347:   \(S\ge0\), and, for every residue \(r\bmod D\), an exponent
1348:   \(s_r\in\{-\infty,0,\dots,S\}\) and a constant \(d_r\ge0\) such that
1349:   \(s_r\ge0\) and \(d_r>0\) exactly on the \(\lambda\)-active residue
1350:   classes, while \(s_r=-\infty\) and \(d_r=0\) on all other residue
1351:   classes. On every residue class
1352:   \[
1353:     z_m
1354:     =
1355:     \lambda^m
1356:     \left(
1357:       d_r m^{s_r}
1358:       +O(m^{s_r-1})
1359:     \right)
1360:     +O(\theta^m m^S),
1361:     \qquad m\equiv r\pmod D .
1362:   \]
1363:   The convention is that the \(O(m^{-1})\) term is absent when
1364:   \(s_r=0\), and the displayed polynomial part is absent when
1365:   \(s_r=-\infty\). Thus non-\(\lambda\)-active residue classes, even
1366:   those with infinitely many accepted lengths, are absorbed into the
1367:   sub-Perron error term.
1368: 
1369:   For every deterministic automaton \(\mathcal A\) over \(\Sigma\) and
1370:   \[
1371:     a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)
1372:     :=\abs{L(\mathcal A)\cap L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m},
1373:   \]
1374:   after replacing \(D,S,\theta\) by common choices, there are
1375:   constants \(e_r\ge0\) and exponents
1376:   \(t_r\in\{-\infty,0,\dots,S\}\) such that on every residue class
1377:   \[
1378:     a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)
1379:     =
1380:     \lambda^m
1381:     \left(
1382:       e_r m^{t_r}
1383:       +O(m^{t_r-1})
1384:     \right)
1385:     +O(\theta^m m^S),
1386:   \]
1387:   with the same convention when \(t_r=0\); if the intersection has
1388:   exponential growth \(<\lambda\) on that residue, then \(t_r=-\infty\)
1389:   and the first displayed polynomial part is absent. Moreover, whenever
1390:   \(r\bmod D\) is \(\lambda\)-active, either \(t_r=-\infty\) or
1391:   \(t_r\le s_r\), and if \(t_r=s_r\) then \(0\le e_r\le d_r\).
1392: 
1393:   Consequently, let \(T_m\subseteq L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m\) satisfy
1394:   \[
1395:     \alpha\frac{\lambda^m}{m}
1396:     \le \abs{T_m}
1397:     \le \beta\frac{\lambda^m}{m}
1398:   \]
1399:   for all sufficiently large \(m\) in every \(\lambda\)-active residue
1400:   class, with fixed \(\alpha,\beta>0\). For every fixed deterministic automaton
1401:   \(\mathcal A\) there is a constant
1402:   \(C_{\mathcal A,\mathcal N,T}>0\) such that
1403:   \[
1404:     \abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m)\triangle T_m}
1405:     \ge
1406:     C_{\mathcal A,\mathcal N,T}\frac{\lambda^m}{m}
1407:   \]
1408:   for all sufficiently large \(m\) in \(\lambda\)-active residue
1409:   classes. On each \(\lambda\)-active residue class either the recall
1410:   against \(T_m\) tends to \(0\) exponentially or polynomially, or else
1411:   the precision is \(O(1/m)\) whenever it is defined.
1412: \end{theorem}
1413: 
1414: \begin{proof}
1415:   Put \(B_N\) in Frobenius normal form. For a finite non-negative
1416:   integer matrix, the rational generating function
1417:   \(u_N^{\mathsf T}(I-xB_N)^{-1}v_N\) has poles at reciprocals of
1418:   eigenvalues of \(B_N\), and the coefficients are finite sums of
1419:   terms \(p_{\mu}(m)\mu^m\), where \(p_\mu\) is a polynomial whose
1420:   degree is one less than the size of the largest Jordan block attached
1421:   to \(\mu\), plus exponentially smaller terms. Equivalently, this is
1422:   the Frobenius normal form or transfer-matrix expansion for regular
1423:   languages; see \cite{SalomaaSoittola1978,BerstelReutenauer2011}.
1424:   For a non-negative matrix, the eigenvalues of modulus \(\lambda\)
1425:   that contribute to accepted paths are roots of unity times
1426:   \(\lambda\), arising from maximal components and chains between them.
1427:   Taking \(D\) to be a common period of those components makes the
1428:   peripheral factors constant on each residue class. On a fixed residue
1429:   \(r\bmod D\), look only at the terms whose exponential factor has
1430:   modulus \(\lambda\). If no such term contributes to accepted paths in
1431:   that residue, then the whole residue-class count has exponential
1432:   growth \(<\lambda\); after enlarging \(\theta\) below \(\lambda\), it
1433:   is included in \(O(\theta^m m^S)\), and we set \(s_r=-\infty\) and
1434:   \(d_r=0\). This case includes residues with infinitely many accepted
1435:   lengths supplied only by smaller components.
1436: 
1437:   If a Perron-scale term does contribute, collect the largest remaining
1438:   polynomial degree as \(s_r\). The leading coefficient \(d_r\) is the
1439:   sum of the non-negative leading contributions of maximal
1440:   component-chains realizing that residue at that degree. This sum is
1441:   positive, because at least one such Perron-scale chain contributes
1442:   and no cancellation is possible for counts of paths in a
1443:   non-negative matrix. These are precisely the \(\lambda\)-active
1444:   residues, and the displayed expansion for \(z_m\) follows.
1445: 
1446:   Form the synchronous product of \(\mathcal N\) with \(\mathcal A\),
1447:   trim unreachable or non-coaccessible states, and let \(B'\) be its
1448:   adjacency matrix. Its accepted-word count is
1449:   \(a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)=u'^{\mathsf T}(B')^m v'\), so the same
1450:   transfer-matrix expansion applies. Its exponential growth rate is at
1451:   most \(\lambda\), because every accepted product word projects
1452:   injectively to a word of \(L(\mathcal N)\). If the product growth rate
1453:   is \(<\lambda\) on a residue class, it is absorbed into
1454:   \(O(\theta^m m^S)\) after increasing \(\theta\) below \(\lambda\) and
1455:   increasing \(S\). If the product has growth \(\lambda\), its leading
1456:   polynomial degree on a \(\lambda\)-active residue cannot exceed \(s_r\);
1457:   otherwise \(a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)\le z_m\) would fail along that
1458:   residue for large \(m\). The same inequality gives \(e_r\le d_r\)
1459:   when \(t_r=s_r\).
1460: 
1461:   Fix a \(\lambda\)-active residue class and put
1462:   \(A_m=L(\mathcal A)\cap L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m\). If
1463:   \(t_r=-\infty\), then \(\abs{A_m}=O(\theta^m m^S)\), and hence
1464:   \[
1465:     \frac{\abs{A_m\cap T_m}}{\abs{T_m}}
1466:     \le
1467:     O(m^{S+1}(\theta/\lambda)^m),
1468:   \]
1469:   so recall decays exponentially and
1470:   \(\abs{A_m\triangle T_m}\ge(\alpha/2)\lambda^m/m\) for all large
1471:   \(m\) in the residue.
1472: 
1473:   Suppose next that the first non-zero leading term in the expansion of
1474:   \(a_m(\mathcal A,\mathcal N)\) on this residue is
1475:   \(\gamma_r\lambda^m m^{u_r}\), with \(\gamma_r>0\) and \(u_r\ge0\).
1476:   Then, for all large \(m\) in the residue,
1477:   \(\abs{A_m}\ge(\gamma_r/2)\lambda^m m^{u_r}\). Since
1478:   \(\abs{T_m}\le\beta\lambda^m/m\),
1479:   \[
1480:     \abs{A_m\triangle T_m}
1481:     \ge \abs{A_m}-\abs{T_m}
1482:     \ge \frac{\gamma_r}{4}\lambda^m m^{u_r}
1483:     \ge \frac{\gamma_r}{4}\frac{\lambda^m}{m},
1484:   \]
1485:   after enlarging the threshold. Whenever \(\abs{A_m}>0\),
1486:   \[
1487:     \frac{\abs{A_m\cap T_m}}{\abs{A_m}}
1488:     \le
1489:     \frac{2\beta}{\gamma_r}\frac{1}{m^{u_r+1}}
1490:     \le
1491:     \frac{2\beta}{\gamma_r}\frac1m .
1492:   \]
1493:   If no such non-zero polynomial term exists, the residue is in the
1494:   exponentially smaller case already handled. There are only finitely
1495:   many \(\lambda\)-active residue classes, so taking the minimum of the
1496:   positive constants obtained above proves the uniform finite-budget
1497:   bound. The theorem makes no prime-scale conclusion on
1498:   non-\(\lambda\)-active residues, where \(L(\mathcal N)\) itself has
1499:   sub-Perron growth and a target of size \(\asymp\lambda^m/m\) cannot
1500:   be a subset of \(L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m\) for all large \(m\).
1501: \end{proof}
1502: 
1503: No additional numeration-system formalism is needed for the next
1504: theorem beyond such a trim deterministic presentation whose underlying
1505: directed graph is strongly connected. In the applications below
1506: \(\mathcal N\) is the canonical presentation of a regular numeration
1507: system. Write
1508: \[
1509:   \mathcal N_m:=L(\mathcal N)\cap\Sigma^m,
1510:   \qquad
1511:   z_m:=\abs{\mathcal N_m}.
1512: \]
1513: Let \(B_N\) be the adjacency matrix of \(\mathcal N\), let
1514: \(\lambda:=\rho(B_N)>1\) be its spectral radius, and let \(d_N\ge 1\)
1515: be the period of \(B_N\).
1516:

\section{Classical Analytic Context}
\label{sec:analytic}

This appendix is purely contextual and may be omitted without changing
any main result. The finite-state arguments use only transfer-matrix
counting and explicit prime-counting bounds. Two classical analytic
contrasts are worth recording: prime support in Euler products often
forces natural boundaries, and scalar finite-matrix determinant models
become \(2\pi i\)-periodic after the substitution \(z=e^{-s}\). Neither
statement is used in
\Cref{thm:effective-fixed-dfa-prime-obstruction,thm:multicomponent-finite-budget}.

\begin{theorem}[Classical: prime-supported Euler products have a natural boundary]
  \label{thm:euler-product-natural-boundary}
  Let \((b_n)_{n\ge1}\subseteq\ZZ_{\ge0}\), assume that \(b_p>0\) for
  infinitely many primes \(p\), and suppose that
  \[
    Z_b(z):=\prod_{n\ge1}(1-z^n)^{-b_n}
  \]
  converges to a holomorphic function on \(\abs z<1\). Then
  \(\abs z=1\) is a natural boundary for \(Z_b\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  This is the prime-supported case of Estermann's natural-boundary
  theorem for Euler products with non-negative integral exponents
  \cite{Estermann1928}; see also the P\'olya--Carlson formulation
  \cite{Polya1923,Carlson1921}. Under the displayed hypotheses,
  infinitely many prime-indexed primitive factors occur with positive
  exponent, so Estermann's theorem applies directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Classical determinant comparison]
  \label{prop:finite-zeta-imaginary-periodicity}
  If \(M\) is a finite square complex matrix, the scalar determinant
  model, with scalar exponential multiplying the matrix,
  \[
    F_M(s):=\det(I-(e^{-s})M)^{-1}
  \]
  is \(2\pi i\)-periodic wherever it is holomorphic. Therefore it
  cannot agree on a non-empty right-half-plane open set with a
  nonconstant ordinary Dirichlet series such as the Riemann
  \(\zeta\)-function.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  The periodicity is the identity
  \[
    F_M(s+2\pi i)
    =\det(I-(e^{-(s+2\pi i)})M)^{-1}
    =F_M(s).
  \]
  If \(F(s)=\sum_{n\ge1}a_nn^{-s}\) is an ordinary Dirichlet series with
  some non-zero coefficient at \(n\ge2\), then
  \[
    G(s):=F(s+2\pi i)-F(s)
      =\sum_{n\ge1}a_n(e^{-2\pi i\log n}-1)n^{-s}
  \]
  has a non-zero first coefficient, where \(\log\) is the real
  logarithm. The usual uniqueness lemma for ordinary Dirichlet series,
  obtained by multiplying by the first index to the power \(\sigma\)
  and letting \(\sigma\to+\infty\), gives \(G(\sigma)\ne0\) for all
  sufficiently large real \(\sigma\). Thus \(F\) is not
  \(2\pi i\)-periodic, and the identity theorem prevents agreement with
  a scalar determinant model on such an open set.
\end{proof}


[35m[3mcodex[0m[0m
The core proof is not mere computation: the new ingredient is the fixed MCFL pumping orbit periodic modulo every allowed modulus, followed by an S-unit rigidity argument. Its depth is moderate rather than top-tier; after the orbit is established, the topology, escape dichotomy, and Pisot classification are mostly short consequences of classical theorems.

# Verification Before Completion

## Overview

Claiming work is complete without verification is dishonesty, not efficiency.

**Core principle:** Evidence before claims, always.

**Violating the letter of this rule is violating the spirit of this rule.**

## The Iron Law

```
NO COMPLETION CLAIMS WITHOUT FRESH VERIFICATION EVIDENCE
```

If you haven't run the verification command in this message, you cannot claim it passes.

## The Gate Function

```
BEFORE claiming any status or expressing satisfaction:

1. IDENTIFY: What command proves this claim?
2. RUN: Execute the FULL command (fresh, complete)
3. READ: Full output, check exit code, count failures
4. VERIFY: Does output confirm the claim?
   - If NO: State actual status with evidence
   - If YES: State claim WITH evidence
5. ONLY THEN: Make the claim

Skip any step = lying, not verifying
```

## Common Failures

| Claim | Requires | Not Sufficient |
|-------|----------|----------------|
| Tests pass | Test command output: 0 failures | Previous run, "should pass" |
| Linter clean | Linter output: 0 errors | Partial check, extrapolation |
| Build succeeds | Build command: exit 0 | Linter passing, logs look good |
| Bug fixed | Test original symptom: passes | Code changed, assumed fixed |
| Regression test works | Red-green cycle verified | Test passes once |
| Agent completed | VCS diff shows changes | Agent reports "success" |
| Requirements met | Line-by-line checklist | Tests passing |

## Red Flags - STOP

- Using "should", "probably", "seems to"
- Expressing satisfaction before verification ("Great!", "Perfect!", "Done!", etc.)
- About to commit/push/PR without verification
- Trusting agent success reports
- Relying on partial verification
- Thinking "just this once"
- Tired and wanting work over
- **ANY wording implying success without having run verification**

## Rationalization Prevention

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Should work now" | RUN the verification |
| "I'm confident" | Confidence â‰  evidence |
| "Just this once" | No exceptions |
| "Linter passed" | Linter â‰  compiler |
| "Agent said success" | Verify independently |
| "I'm tired" | Exhaustion â‰  excuse |
| "Partial check is enough" | Partial proves nothing |
| "Different words so rule doesn't apply" | Spirit over letter |

## Key Patterns

**Tests:**
```
âœ… [Run test command] [See: 34/34 pass] "All tests pass"
âŒ "Should pass now" / "Looks correct"
```

**Regression tests (TDD Red-Green):**
```
âœ… Write â†’ Run (pass) â†’ Revert fix â†’ Run (MUST FAIL) â†’ Restore â†’ Run (pass)
âŒ "I've written a regression test" (without red-green verification)
```

**Build:**
```
âœ… [Run build] [See: exit 0] "Build passes"
âŒ "Linter passed" (linter doesn't check compilation)
```

**Requirements:**
```
âœ… Re-read plan â†’ Create checklist â†’ Verify each â†’ Report gaps or completion
âŒ "Tests pass, phase complete"
```

**Agent delegation:**
```
âœ… Agent reports success â†’ Check VCS diff â†’ Verify changes â†’ Report actual state
âŒ Trust agent report
```

## Why This Matters

From 24 failure memories:
- your human partner said "I don't believe you" - trust broken
- Undefined functions shipped - would crash
- Missing requirements shipped - incomplete features
- Time wasted on false completion â†’ redirect â†’ rework
- Violates: "Honesty is a core value. If you lie, you'll be replaced."

## When To Apply

**ALWAYS before:**
- ANY variation of success/completion claims
- ANY expression of satisfaction
- ANY positive statement about work state
- Committing, PR creation, task completion
- Moving to next task
- Delegating to agents

**Rule applies to:**
- Exact phrases
- Paraphrases and synonyms
- Implications of success
- ANY communication suggesting completion/correctness

## The Bottom Line

**No shortcuts for verification.**

Run the command. Read the output. THEN claim the result.

This is non-negotiable.

[35m[3mcodex[0m[0m
1. **HEADLINE RESULT.** For a standard linear Pisot \(U\)-system with dominant root \(\beta\), an infinite MCFL with bounded prime support outside the primes dividing the minimal tail constant exists if and only if \(\beta\) is an integer; consequently, for nonintegral \(\beta\), every infinite MCFL has unbounded prime support outside those norm primes. **Label:** `cor:linear-pisot-bounded-support-classification`.

2. **IS IT ACTUALLY NEW? GENUINELY NEW.** Hartmanis–Shank, “On the Recognition of Primes by Automata” (JACM, 1968), and Schützenberger, “A Remark on Acceptable Sets of Numbers” (JACM, 1968), prove fixed-base prime CF-immunity, but not MCFL immunity, Pisot numeration, or bounded-support classification. Seki–Matsumura–Fujii–Kasami (TCS, 1991) provide the weak MCFL pumping lemma and Evertse, “On sums of \(S\)-units and linear recurrences” (Compos. Math., 1984), provides the recurrence rigidity, but I found no prior work combining them into this theorem.

3. **DEPTH OF THE MATHEMATICS.** Moderate and substantive, not bookkeeping: the modulus-independent synchronized pumping orbit is a real mechanism, and the Evertse–Schur reduction to an exact geometric subsequence is nontrivial; however, no new hard estimate or deep arithmetic theorem is proved, and most topological and divisibility-tree conclusions are short consequences once the orbit exists.

4. **TOP-JOURNAL VERDICT. TIER-3.** Plausible for a solid specialist journal such as Monatshefte, but the restricted numeration class, dependence on classical heavy inputs, and proliferation of downstream corollaries leave it below Trans. AMS/ETDS depth.

5. **WHAT WOULD MOVE IT UP ONE TIER.** Prove decidability for every effectively presented regular canonical recurrent system: given a DFA for \(\mathcal R_U\) and the minimal integral tail recurrence \(\mu_U\), decide whether \(\mathcal R_U\) contains a synchronized ray with values \(cb^t\), equivalently whether it contains an infinite MCFL with bounded prime support outside the divisors of \(\mu_U(0)\).

6. **WEAKEST LINK.** `lem:s-unit-increasing-recurrence-rigidity`, and therefore `thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray`: the manuscript compresses the passage from Evertse’s prime-ideal theorem to an exact integer geometric subsequence into a short argument whose handling of degeneracy, residue subsequences, and one-root polynomial factors will require close referee verification; the finite scripts do not test this step.

1. **HEADLINE RESULT.** For a standard linear Pisot \(U\)-system with dominant root \(\beta\), an infinite MCFL with bounded prime support outside the primes dividing the minimal tail constant exists if and only if \(\beta\) is an integer; consequently, for nonintegral \(\beta\), every infinite MCFL has unbounded prime support outside those norm primes. **Label:** `cor:linear-pisot-bounded-support-classification`.

2. **IS IT ACTUALLY NEW? GENUINELY NEW.** Hartmanis–Shank, “On the Recognition of Primes by Automata” (JACM, 1968), and Schützenberger, “A Remark on Acceptable Sets of Numbers” (JACM, 1968), prove fixed-base prime CF-immunity, but not MCFL immunity, Pisot numeration, or bounded-support classification. Seki–Matsumura–Fujii–Kasami (TCS, 1991) provide the weak MCFL pumping lemma and Evertse, “On sums of \(S\)-units and linear recurrences” (Compos. Math., 1984), provides the recurrence rigidity, but I found no prior work combining them into this theorem.

3. **DEPTH OF THE MATHEMATICS.** Moderate and substantive, not bookkeeping: the modulus-independent synchronized pumping orbit is a real mechanism, and the Evertse–Schur reduction to an exact geometric subsequence is nontrivial; however, no new hard estimate or deep arithmetic theorem is proved, and most topological and divisibility-tree conclusions are short consequences once the orbit exists.

4. **TOP-JOURNAL VERDICT. TIER-3.** Plausible for a solid specialist journal such as Monatshefte, but the restricted numeration class, dependence on classical heavy inputs, and proliferation of downstream corollaries leave it below Trans. AMS/ETDS depth.

5. **WHAT WOULD MOVE IT UP ONE TIER.** Prove decidability for every effectively presented regular canonical recurrent system: given a DFA for \(\mathcal R_U\) and the minimal integral tail recurrence \(\mu_U\), decide whether \(\mathcal R_U\) contains a synchronized ray with values \(cb^t\), equivalently whether it contains an infinite MCFL with bounded prime support outside the divisors of \(\mu_U(0)\).

6. **WEAKEST LINK.** `lem:s-unit-increasing-recurrence-rigidity`, and therefore `thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray`: the manuscript compresses the passage from Evertse’s prime-ideal theorem to an exact integer geometric subsequence into a short argument whose handling of degeneracy, residue subsequences, and one-root polynomial factors will require close referee verification; the finite scripts do not test this step.
